Substitutive structure of Jeandel-Rao aperiodic tilings
Sébastien Labbé

Abstract
. Jeandel and Rao proved that 11 is the size of the smallest set of Wang tiles, i.e., unit squares with colored edges, that admit valid tilings (contiguous edges of adjacent tiles have the same color) of the plane, none of them being invariant under a nontrivial translation. We study herein the Wang shift Ω 0 \Omega_{0} made of all valid tilings using the set 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} of 11 aperiodic Wang tiles discovered by Jeandel and Rao. We show that there exists a minimal subshift X 0 X_{0} of Ω 0 \Omega_{0} such that every tiling in X 0 X_{0} can be decomposed uniquely into 19 distinct patches of sizes ranging from 45 to 112 that are equivalent to a set of 19 self-similar and aperiodic Wang tiles. We suggest that this provides an almost complete description of the substitutive structure of Jeandel-Rao tilings, as we believe that Ω 0 ∖ X 0 \Omega_{0}\setminus X_{0} is a null set for any shift-invariant probability measure on Ω 0 \Omega_{0} . The proof is based on 12 elementary steps, 10 of which involve the same procedure allowing one to desubstitute Wang tilings from the existence of a subset of marker tiles. The 2 other steps involve the addition of decorations to deal with fault lines and changing the base of the ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}^{2} -action through a shear conjugacy. Algorithms are provided to find markers, recognizable substitutions, and shear conjugacy from a set of Wang tiles.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 52C23; Secondary 37B50

1.
Introduction

Aperiodic tilings are much studied for their beauty but also for their links
with various aspects of science including dynamical systems
[
Sol97
]
,
topology
[
Sad08
]
, number theory
[
BR10
]
,
theoretical computer science
[
Jea17
]

and crystallography.
Chapters 10 and 11 of
[
GS87
]
and the more recent
book on aperiodic order
[
BG13
]
give an excellent overview of what is
known on aperiodic tilings.
The first examples of aperiodic tilings were
tilings of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by

Wang tiles
, that is, unit square tiles with colored edges

[
Ber65
,
Knu69
,
Rob71
,
Kar96
,
Cul96
,
Oll08
]
.
A tiling by Wang tiles is
valid
if every contiguous edges have the same color.
The set of all valid tilings
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
using translated copies of a finite set
\mathcal{T}
of Wang tiles
is called the
Wang shift
of
\mathcal{T}
and denoted
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
(rotated or reflected copies of tiles are not allowed).
It is a
2
-dimensional
subshift
as it
is invariant under translations called
shifts
and closed under taking limits.
A nonempty Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is said to be
aperiodic
if none of its
tilings have a nontrivial period.

2

Figure 1.
The Jeandel-Rao’s set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of 11 Wang tiles.

Jeandel and Rao
[
JR15
]

proved that the set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of Wang tiles shown in Figure
1

is the smallest possible set of Wang tiles that is aperiodic.
Indeed, based on computer explorations, they proved
that every set of Wang tiles of cardinality
\leq 10
either admits a
periodic tiling of the plane or does not tile the plane at all. Thus there is
no aperiodic set of Wang tiles of cardinality less than or equal to 10. In the same
work, they found this interesting candidate of cardinality 11 and they proved
that the associated Wang shift
\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}
is aperiodic.
Their proof is based on the representation of a set of Wang tiles as a
transducer and on the
description of a sequence of
transducers describing larger and larger infinite horizontal strips of Wang
tiles by iteratively taking products of transducers.

Their example is also minimal for the number of colors.
Indeed it is known that three colors are not enough to allow an aperiodic
set of tiles
[
CHLL12
]
and
Jeandel and Rao mentioned in their
preprint that
while they used 5 colors for the edges
\{0,1,2,3,4\}
,
they claim colors 0 and 4 can be merged without losing the aperiodic property.
In this work, we keep colors 0 and 4 as distinct and we associate the number 4
to the color gray in figures.

In this contribution, we show the existence of a minimal
aperiodic subshift
X_{0}
of the Jeandel-Rao tilings
\Omega_{0}
.
The substitutive structure of
X_{0}
can be described in terms
of a minimal, aperiodic and self-similar Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}

based on a set of 19 Wang tiles
\mathcal{U}
introduced in

[
Lab19a
]
.
Our main result can be summarized as follows.

Theorem 1
.

There exists an aperiodic and minimal subshift
X_{0}
of the Jeandel-Rao
tilings
\Omega_{0}
such that any tiling in
X_{0}
can be decomposed uniquely
into 19 distinct patches (two of size 45, four of size 70, six of size 72
and seven of size 112) that are equivalent to the set of 19 Wang
tiles
\mathcal{U}
.

We show that
\Omega_{0}\setminus X_{0}\neq\varnothing
in
Proposition
35
due to the presence of some horizontal fault
lines in
\Omega_{0}
, but we believe that
X_{0}
gives an almost complete
description of the Jeandel-Rao tilings.
More precisely and as opposed to the minimal subshift of
the Kari-Culik tilings
[
Sie17
]
,
we think the following holds.

Conjecture 2
.

\Omega_{0}\setminus X_{0}
is of measure zero
for any shift-invariant probability measure on
\Omega_{0}
.

Figure 2.
A finite part of a Jeandel-Rao aperiodic tiling in
\Omega_{0}
. Any
tiling in the minimal subshift
X_{0}
of
\Omega_{0}
that we describe can be
decomposed uniquely into 19 supertiles (two of size 45,
four of size 70,
six of size 72
and seven of size 112). The thick black lines show the
contour of the supertiles. The figure illustrates two complete supertiles
of size 72 and 45, respectively. While Jeandel-Rao set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is not self-similar,
the 19 supertiles are self-similar.

The decomposition of a tiling in
X_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
into 19 distinct patches
is illustrated in Figure
2
.
All of the 19 patches and their link with the Wang tiles in
\mathcal{U}
are shown in
Figure
19
in the appendix.
The 19 patches are complicated objects, but we provide a systematic construction
of them by means of a product of substitutions where each substitution
(or
2
-dimensional morphism)
represents an elementary step in the procedure. The substitutions
are computed automatically using the same algorithms at each step.
There are a dozens of such elementary steps between Jeandel-Rao tilings

X_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
that are illustrated in
Figure
3
.

\Omega_{0}
\Omega_{1}
\Omega_{2}
\Omega_{3}
\Omega_{4}

X_{2}
X_{3}

\Omega_{5}
\Omega_{6}
\Omega_{7}
\Omega_{8}
\Omega_{9}
\Omega_{10}
\Omega_{11}
\Omega_{12}
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
\subset
\supset
\supset
\supset
\supset
\omega_{0}
\omega_{1}
\omega_{2}
\omega_{3}
\omega_{0}
\omega_{1}
\omega_{2}
\omega_{3}
\jmath

\omega_{6}
\omega_{7}
\omega_{8}
\omega_{9}
\omega_{10}
\omega_{11}
\rho
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}

Figure 3.
Substitutive structure of Jeandel-Rao aperiodic Wang shift

\Omega_{0}
and its minimal subshift
X_{0}
leading to the self-similar
aperiodic and minimal Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
introduced in

[
Lab19a
]
.

The substitutive structure of Jeandel-Rao tilings
is given by
a sequence of sets of Wang tiles
\{\mathcal{T}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 12}

together with their associated Wang shifts
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 12}
.
The cardinality of the involved sets of tiles is in the table below.

\begin{array}[]{l|cccccccccccccc}\text{Set of tiles}&\mathcal{T}_{0}&\mathcal{T}_{1}&\mathcal{T}_{2}&\mathcal{T}_{3}&\mathcal{T}_{4}&\mathcal{T}_{5}&\mathcal{T}_{6}&\mathcal{T}_{7}&\mathcal{T}_{8}&\mathcal{T}_{9}&\mathcal{T}_{10}&\mathcal{T}_{11}&\mathcal{T}_{12}&\mathcal{U}\\
\hline\cr\text{Cardinality}&11&13&20&24&28&29&29&20&20&22&18&21&19&19\end{array}

The morphism
\omega_{0}\,\omega_{1}\,\omega_{2}\,\omega_{3}:\Omega_{4}\to\Omega_{0}
is
shown in Figure
17
.
The map
\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{5}
is shown in
Figure
18
.
The map
\omega_{0}\,\omega_{1}\,\omega_{2}\,\omega_{3}\,\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{0}

is the map which is shown in Figure
19
.
The map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is not onto but its image

X_{4}=\jmath(\Omega_{5})
has an important role in the comprehension of
Jeandel-Rao aperiodic tilings as it leads to the minimal subshift
X_{0}
.
The fact that each tiling in
X_{0}
can be decomposed uniquely
into this procedure follows from the fact that each morphism
\omega_{i}

with
0\leq i\leq 3

or
6\leq i\leq 11

is recognizable and onto up to a shift,
and both
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to X_{4}
and

\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}
are one-to-one and onto.
The term
recognizable morphism
essentially means that the morphism is
one-to-one up to a shift and its precise definition can be found in
Section
2.9
.

The construction of the morphisms
\omega_{i}
is inspired from a well-known method
to study self-similar aperiodic tilings.
One way to prove that a Wang shift is aperiodic is to use the
unique
composition property

[
Sol98
]
also known as

composition-decomposition method

[
BG13
]

(see Proposition
8
).
That method was used in
[
Lab19a
]

to show that
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is self-similar, minimal and aperiodic
based on the notion of marker tiles and recognizability.
The reader may refer to Section
2.8

for the definition of self-similarity.

The same method can be used in the context of Wang shifts that are not
self-similar. The idea is to prove that a Wang shift is similar to another one
which is known to be aperiodic (see
Lemma
9
).
This reminds of
[
BSTY19
]

where the authors study the recognizability for sequences of morphisms
in the theory of
S
-adic systems on
\mathbb{Z}

[
BD14
]
.
Note that applying a sequence of recognizable substitutions in the context of
hierarchical tilings of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
was also considered in

[
FS14
,
Fra18
]
.

Due to the fact that we have many steps to perform to understand Jeandel-Rao tilings, we
improve the method used in
[
Lab19a
]

which,
based on the existence of marker tiles
M\subset\mathcal{T}

among a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
, proved the existence of
a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}
and a
recognizable
2
-dimensional morphism
\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
that is onto up to
a shift.
The improvement is that we provide two algorithms.
Algorithm
1

finds a set of marker tiles
M\subset\mathcal{T}
(if it exists)
from a set of Wang
tiles
\mathcal{T}
and a given surrounding radius to consider (the surrounding radius
input is necessary since otherwise it is undecidable).
Algorithm
2

computes the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}
and the recognizable

2
-dimensional morphism
\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
from a set of markers
M\subset\mathcal{T}
.
The morphism is of the form

\square\mapsto\square,\square\mapsto\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to11.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.87 M 0 7.87 L 0 15.75 L 7.87 15.75 L 7.87 7.87 Z M 7.87 15.75}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

or of the form
\square\mapsto\square,\square\mapsto\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 7.87 L 7.87 7.87 L 7.87 0 Z M 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 0 M 7.87 0 L 7.87 7.87 L 15.75 7.87 L 15.75 0 Z M 15.75 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

mapping each tile from
\mathcal{S}
on a tile in
\mathcal{T}
or on a domino of two tiles in

\mathcal{T}
.

We use these two algorithms to describe the substitutive structure of the Wang
tilings
\Omega_{0}
made with the tiles from Jeandel-Rao set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{0}
.
It turns out that Jeandel-Rao tiles has a subset of markers which allow
to desubstitute uniquely any Jeandel-Rao tiling.
In other words, the two algorithms prove the existence of a set of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}_{1}
, its Wang shift
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}
and
a recognizable
2
-dimensional
morphism
\omega_{0}:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{0}
that is onto up to a shift.
We reapply the same method
again and again and again on the resulting tiles
to get sets of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{2}
,
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
.

The two algorithms stop to work for the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{4}
since it has
no markers.
The first reason is that
\Omega_{4}
has fault lines as it is the case for Robinson tilings

[
Rob71
]
. These fault lines are also present in
\Omega_{0}
as there
exists tilings in
\Omega_{0}
with a biinfinite horizontal sequence of tiles all
of them with color 0 on the bottom edge.
Such a horizontal fault line of 0’s can be seen in
Figure
2
. Translating to the left or to the
right the upper-half tiling above the fault line preserves the validity of
the Wang tiling but breaks the 19 patterns obtained from the substitutive
structure.
This implies that not all tilings in
\Omega_{0}
and in
\Omega_{4}
are the
image of tilings in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
by a sequence of substitutions.
We deal with this issue as done in
[
GJS12
]

by creating a new set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{5}
which adds decorations on tiles
\mathcal{T}_{4}

and a map
\jmath:\mathcal{T}_{5}\to\mathcal{T}_{4}
which removes the decorations.
We prove that
\jmath
is one-to-one on tilings
\Omega_{5}
.
The advantage is that the Wang shift
\Omega_{5}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{5}}
has no fault
lines.

The set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{5}
has no markers neither and the reason is that tilings in
\Omega_{5}
admits diagonal markers instead of marker tiles appearing on rows or columns.
So we introduce a new set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{6}
such that tilings in
\Omega_{6}

are tilings in
\Omega_{5}
sheared by the action of the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{-1}
.

The set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{6}
has markers, thus we can reapply the two algorithms
again. We get a new set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{7}
and a recognizable morphism

\Omega_{7}\to\Omega_{6}
that is onto up to a shift.
We apply this method five more times to get the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and its
Wang shift
\Omega_{12}
.
We prove that the Wang shift
\Omega_{12}
is equivalent to

\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
where
\mathcal{U}
is a set of 19 Wang tiles which was
introduced in
[
Lab19a
]
.
It was proved that the Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}

is self-similar, aperiodic and minimal
as there exists an expansive and primitive morphism

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
that is
recognizable and onto up to a shift.

The statement of the main result of this contribution on the substitutive
structure of aperiodic Jeandel-Rao Wang tilings is below
in which we use the following notation

{\overline{X}}^{\sigma}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}X=\bigcup_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\{\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}(x)\mid x\in X\}

for the closure of a set
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
under the shift
\sigma
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\Omega_{0}
be the Jeandel-Rao Wang shift.
There exist sets of Wang tiles
\{\mathcal{T}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 12}

together with their associated Wang shifts
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 12}

that provide the substitutive structure of Jeandel-Rao tilings.
More precisely,

(1)

There exists a sequence of recognizable
2
-dimensional morphisms:

\Omega_{0}\xleftarrow{\omega_{0}}\Omega_{1}\xleftarrow{\omega_{1}}\Omega_{2}\xleftarrow{\omega_{2}}\Omega_{3}\xleftarrow{\omega_{3}}\Omega_{4}

that are onto up to a shift, i.e.,

\overline{\omega_{i}(\Omega_{i+1})}^{\sigma}=\Omega_{i}

for each
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

(2)

There exists an embedding
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
which is a
topological conjugacy onto its image.

(3)

There exists a shear conjugacy

\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}

which shears Wang tilings by the action of the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

(4)

There exists a sequence of recognizable
2
-dimensional morphisms:

\Omega_{6}\xleftarrow{\omega_{6}}\Omega_{7}\xleftarrow{\omega_{7}}\Omega_{8}\xleftarrow{\omega_{8}}\Omega_{9}\xleftarrow{\omega_{9}}\Omega_{10}\xleftarrow{\omega_{10}}\Omega_{11}\xleftarrow{\omega_{11}}\Omega_{12}

that are onto up to a shift, i.e.,

\overline{\omega_{i}(\Omega_{i+1})}^{\sigma}=\Omega_{i}

for each
i\in\{6,7,8,9,10,11\}
.

(5)

The Wang shift
\Omega_{12}
is equivalent to
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
, thus is
self-similar, aperiodic and minimal.

This result has the following consequences.

Corollary 4
.

\Omega_{i}
is aperiodic and minimal
for every
i
with
5\leq i\leq 12
.

Let
X_{4}=\jmath(\Omega_{5})
be the image of the embedding
\jmath
,
as well as

X_{3}=\overline{\omega_{3}(X_{4})}^{\sigma}
,

X_{2}=\overline{\omega_{2}(X_{3})}^{\sigma}
,

X_{1}=\overline{\omega_{1}(X_{2})}^{\sigma}
and

X_{0}=\overline{\omega_{0}(X_{1})}^{\sigma}
.

Corollary 5
.

X_{i}\subseteq\Omega_{i}
is an aperiodic and minimal subshift of
\Omega_{i}

for every
i
with
0\leq i\leq 4
.

Structure of the article

After the introduction and the preliminaries,
the article is structured into four parts.
In Part
I
,
we recall the definition of markers, the
desubstitution of Wang shifts and we propose algorithms to compute them
and
we desubstitute Jeandel-Rao tilings from
\Omega_{0}
to
\Omega_{4}
.
In Part
II
,
we construct the set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{5}
by adding decorations to tiles
\mathcal{T}_{4}
to avoid
fault lines in tilings of
\Omega_{4}
, we prove that

\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is an embedding and
we construct the shear conjugacy
\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}
.
In Part
III

we desubstitute tilings from
\Omega_{6}
to
\Omega_{12}

and
we prove the main results.
In Part
IV
,
we examine the set
\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}

and show that Jeandel-Rao tilings are not minimal.

Code

All computations based on SageMath
[
Sag19
]
with
slabbe

optional package
[
Lab19b
]

associated to this contribution are gathered in a SageMath Jupyter
notebook
[
Lab19c
]
.

2.
Preliminaries on Wang tilings and
d
-dimensional words

In this section, we introduce
subshifts, shifts of finite type,
Wang tiles,
fusion of Wang tiles,
the transducer representation of Wang tiles,

d
-dimensional words, morphisms, and languages.
It is mostly the same as the preliminary section of

[
Lab19a
]
.

We denote by
\mathbb{Z}=\{\dots,-1,0,1,2,\dots\}
the integers and
by
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
the nonnegative integers.

2.1.
Subshifts and shifts of finite type

We follow the notations of
[
Sch01
]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite set,
d\geq 1
, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be the set of all maps

x:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathcal{A}
, furnished with the
product topology of the discrete topology on
\mathcal{A}
.

We write a typical point
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
as
x=(x_{\mathbf{m}})=(x_{\mathbf{m}}:\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d})
,
where
x_{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{A}
denotes the value of
x
at
\mathbf{m}
.
The topology
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is compatible with the metric
\delta
defined for all

x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
by
\delta(x,x^{\prime})=2^{-\min\left\{|\mathbf{n}|\,:\,x_{\mathbf{n}}\neq x^{\prime}_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}}
.
The
shift action

\sigma:\mathbf{n}\mapsto\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is defined by

(1)

(\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x))_{\mathbf{m}}=x_{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}}

for every
x=(x_{\mathbf{m}})\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. A subset
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is
shift-invariant
if
\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(X)=X
for every

\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and a closed, shift-invariant subset
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a

subshift
.
If
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a subshift we write

\sigma
for the restriction of the shift-action
(
1
) to
X
.
If
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a subshift it will sometimes be helpful to specify the
shift-action of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
explicitly and to write
(X,\sigma)
instead of
X
.
A subshift
(X,\sigma)
is called
minimal
if
X
does not
contain any nonempty, proper, closed shift-invariant subset.

For any subset
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we denote by
\pi_{S}:\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\to\mathcal{A}^{S}
the
projection map which restricts every
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
to
S
.
A
pattern
is a function
p:S\to\mathcal{A}
for some finite subset

S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
A subshift
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a

shift of finite type
(SFT) if there exists a finite set
\mathcal{F}

of
forbidden
patterns such that

(2)

X=\{x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mid\pi_{S}\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)\notin\mathcal{F}\text{ for every }\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{ and }S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}.

In this case, we write
X=\textsc{SFT}(\mathcal{F})
.

We adapt the definition of conjugacy of dynamical systems from

[
LM95
, p. 185]
to subshifts.
Let
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
Y\subset\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be two subshifts.
A
homomorphism

\theta:(X,\sigma)\to(Y,\sigma)
is a continuous function

\theta:X\to Y
satisfying the commuting property
that
\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\circ\theta=\theta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}
for every
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
A homomorphism is called an
embedding

if it is one-to-one, a
factor map
if it is onto, and a
topological
conjugacy
if it is both one-to-one and onto and its inverse map is continuous.
Two subshifts are
topologically conjugate
if there is a topological
conjugacy between them.

If
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
Y\subset\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
are two subshifts,
we say that a
\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
-homomorphism

\theta:(X,\sigma)\to(Y,\sigma)
is a continuous function

\theta:X\to Y
satisfying the commuting property
that

\sigma^{M\mathbf{k}}\circ\theta=\theta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}

for every
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for some matrix
M\in\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
.
A
\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
-homomorphism is called an
\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
-conjugacy
if it is
both one-to-one and onto and its inverse map is continuous.
A
\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
-conjugacy is called an
shear conjugacy

if the matrix
M\in\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
is a shear matrix.
The notion of
\textrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
-conjugacy corresponds to
flip-conjugacy
when

d=1

[
BT98
]

and to
extended symmetry
when
X=Y

[
BRY18
]
.

2.2.
Wang tiles

A
Wang tile

\tau=\raisebox{-8.53581pt}{

{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
is a unit square with colored edges
formally represented as a tuple of four colors
(a,b,c,d)\in I\times J\times I\times J

where
I
,
J
are
two finite sets (the vertical and horizontal colors respectively).
For each Wang tile
\tau=(a,b,c,d)
, we denote by

\textsc{right}(\tau)=a
,

\textsc{top}(\tau)=b
,

\textsc{left}(\tau)=c
,

\textsc{bottom}(\tau)=d

the colors of the right, top, left, and bottom edges of
\tau

[
Wan61
,
Rob71
]
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles. A
valid tiling of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by
\mathcal{T}
is an assignment
x

of tiles to each position of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
so that contiguous edges have the same
color, that is, it is a function
x:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
satisfying

(3)

\displaystyle\textsc{right}\circ x(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle=\textsc{left}\circ x(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{1})

(4)

\displaystyle\textsc{top}\circ x(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle=\textsc{bottom}\circ x(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{2})

for every
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

where
\mathbf{e}_{1}=(1,0)
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}=(0,1)
.
We denote by
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
the set of all valid Wang tilings of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}

by
\mathcal{T}
and we call it the
Wang shift
of
\mathcal{T}
. It is a SFT of
the form (
2
) since there exists a finite set
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden patterns
made of all horizontal and vertical dominoes of two tiles that do not share an
edge of the same color.

A set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
of cardinality
n\in\mathbb{N}
will be written as a set

\{\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\dots\tau_{n-1}\}
. Using the obvious bijection between the
sets
\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
and
\mathcal{T}
, any tiling
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
can be seen as a
map
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
and vice versa.

A set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}

tiles
the plane if
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\neq\varnothing

and
does not tile
the plane if
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}=\varnothing
.
A tiling
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is
periodic
if there is a nonzero period

\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
such that
x=\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)

and otherwise it is said
nonperiodic
.
A set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
is
periodic
if there is a tiling

x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
which is periodic. A set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
is
aperiodic
if

\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\neq\varnothing
and every tiling
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is nonperiodic.
As explained in the first page of
[
Rob71
]

(see also
[
BG13
, Prop. 5.9]
),
if
\mathcal{T}
is periodic, then there is a tiling
x
by
\mathcal{T}
with two linearly
independent translation vectors (in particular a tiling
x
with vertical
and horizontal translation vectors).

We say that two sets of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{S}
are

equivalent
if there exist two bijections
i:I\to I^{\prime}

j:J\to J^{\prime}
such
that

\mathcal{S}=\{(i(a),j(b),i(c),j(d))\mid(a,b,c,d)\in\mathcal{T}\}.

that share an edge in a tiling according to
Equations
3
and
4
.

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.2932pt}{18.7865pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad\text{ if }B=C.

If
Y\neq W
, we say that
u\boxbar v
is
not defined
.
Similarly, if
B\neq C
, we say that
u\boxminus v
is
not defined
.
In this contribution, we may assume that the operation
\cdot
is
associative so we always denote it implicitly by concatenation of colors.

In what follows, we propose algorithms and results that works for both
operations
\boxbar
and
\boxminus
.
It is thus desirable to have a common notation to denote both, so we define

u\boxslash^{1}v=u\boxbar v\qquad\text{ and }\qquad u\boxslash^{2}v=u\boxminus v.

If
u\boxslash^{i}v
is defined for some
i\in\{1,2\}
, it means that tiles
u

and
v
can appear at position
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
in a tiling for some

\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, one can define a new set of tiles from
two set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{S}
as

\mathcal{T}\boxslash^{i}\mathcal{S}=\{u\boxslash^{i}v\text{ defined }\mid u\in\mathcal{T},v\in\mathcal{S}\}.

2.4.
Transducer representation of Wang tiles

A transducer
M
is a labeled directed graph whose nodes are called

states
and edges are called
transitions
. The transitions are
labeled by pairs
a|b
of letters. The first letter
a
is the input symbol and
the second letter
b
is the output symbol. There is no initial nor final state.
A transducer
M
computes a relation
\rho(M)
between bi-infinite sequences of
letters.

As observed in
[
Kar96
]
and extensively used in

[
JR15
]
, any finite set of Wang tiles may be interpreted
as a transducer. To a given set of tiles
\mathcal{T}
, the states of the corresponding
transducer
M_{\mathcal{T}}
are the colors of the vertical edges. The colors of
horizontal edges are the input and output symbols. There is a transition from
state
s
to state
t
with label
a|b
if and only if there is a tile

(t,b,s,a)\in\mathcal{T}
whose left, right, bottom and top edges are colored by
s
,

t
,
a
and
b
, respectively:

M_{\mathcal{T}}=\left\{s\xrightarrow{a|b}t:\raisebox{-8.53581pt}{
\hbox to26.01pt{\vbox to26.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 35.43 L 0 35.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=(t,b,s,a)\in\mathcal{T}\right\}.

The Jeandel-Rao set of Wang tiles defined in
Figure
1
can be seen as a transducer with 4
states and 11 transitions consisting of two connected components
T_{0}
and

T_{1}
(see Figure
4
).

T_{1}:

Figure 4.
The Jeandel-Rao set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{0}
seen as a transducer
M_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}
.
Each tile of
\mathcal{T}_{0}
corresponds to a transition.
For example, the first tile in Figure
1

is associated to the transition
2\xrightarrow{1|4}2
, etc.
The transducer
M_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}

consists of two connected components identified as
T_{0}
and
T_{1}

in
[
JR15
]
.

Sequences
x
and
y
are in the relation
\rho(M_{\mathcal{T}})
if and only
if there exists a row of tiles, with matching vertical edges, whose bottom edges
form the sequence
x
and whose top edges form the sequence
y
.
For example, consider the
6\times 1
rectangle tiled with Jeandel-Rao tiles:
3
1
0
2

1
3
3

2
3
2
3
3
1
2
1

3
1
0
2
1
2

The sequence of bottom colors
is
232212
.
Starting in state
0
and reading that word as input in the transducer

M_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}
we follow the transitions:

0\xrightarrow{2|1}3\xrightarrow{3|1}3\xrightarrow{2|2}1\xrightarrow{2|3}3\xrightarrow{1|1}1\xrightarrow{2|2}0

We finish in state
0
and we get
112312
as output which corresponds
to the sequence of top colors of the same row.
In general, there is a
one-to-one correspondence between valid tilings of the plane, and
the execution of the transducer on biinfinite sequences.

Notice that the result of the usual composition of transducers
corresponds to the transducer of
\mathcal{T}\boxslash^{2}\mathcal{S}
:

(5)

M_{\mathcal{T}}\circ M_{\mathcal{S}}=M_{\mathcal{T}\boxslash^{2}\mathcal{S}}.

As done in
[
JR15
]
,
the result of the composition can be filtered by
recursively removing any source or sink state from the transducer
reducing the size of the set of tiles.
This is very helpful for doing computations.

2.5.
d
-dimensional word

In this section, we recall the definition of
d
-dimensional word that appeared
in
[
CKR10
]
and we keep the notation
u\odot^{i}v
they proposed
for the concatenation.

We denote by

\{\mathbf{e}_{k}|1\leq k\leq d\}
the canonical
basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
where
d\geq 1
is an integer.
If
i\leq j
are integers, then
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
denotes the
interval of integers
\{i,i+1,\dots,j\}
.
Let
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
\mathcal{A}
be an alphabet.
We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
the set of functions

u:\llbracket 0,n_{1}-1\rrbracket\times\cdots\times\llbracket 0,n_{d}-1\rrbracket\to\mathcal{A}.

An element
u\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
is called a

d
-dimensional word
u
of shape
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}

on the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
.
We use the notation
\textsc{shape}(u)=\mathbf{n}
when necessary.
The set of all finite
d
-dimensional words is

\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}=\{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}\mid\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\}
.
A
d
-dimensional word of shape
\mathbf{e}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{e}_{i}
is called a

domino in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{k}
.
When the context is clear, we write
\mathcal{A}
instead of
\mathcal{A}^{(1,\dots,1)}
.
When
d=2
, we represent a
d
-dimensional word
u
of shape
(n_{1},n_{2})
as a
matrix with Cartesian coordinates:

u=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}u_{0,n_{2}-1}&\dots&u_{n_{1}-1,n_{2}-1}\\
\dots&\dots&\dots\\
u_{0,0}&\dots&u_{n_{1}-1,0}\end{array}\right).

Let
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
u\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
and
v\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{m}}
.
If there exists an index
i
such that the
shapes
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{m}
are equal except maybe at index
i
,
then the
concatenation of
u
and
v
in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}

is
defined
: it is
the

d
-dimensional word
u\odot^{i}v
of shape
(n_{1},\dots,n_{i-1},n_{i}+m_{i},n_{i+1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}

given as

(u\odot^{i}v)(\mathbf{a})=\begin{cases}u(\mathbf{a})&\text{if}\quad 0\leq a_{i}<n_{i},\\
v(\mathbf{a}-n_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i})&\text{if}\quad n_{i}\leq a_{i}<n_{i}+m_{i}.\end{cases}

If the shapes
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{m}
are not equal except at
index
i
, we say that the concatenation of
u\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
and
v\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{m}}
in
the direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is
not defined
.
The following equation illustrates the concatenation of words in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}
when
d=2
:

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}2&8&7\\
7&3&9\\
1&1&0\\
6&6&7\\
7&4&3\end{array}\right)\odot^{1}\left[\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}4&5\\
10&5\end{array}\right)\odot^{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}3&10\\
9&9\\
0&0\\
\end{array}\right)\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}2&8&7\\
7&3&9\\
1&1&0\\
6&6&7\\
7&4&3\end{array}\right)\odot^{1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}3&10\\
9&9\\
0&0\\
4&5\\
10&5\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}2&8&7&3&10\\
7&3&9&9&9\\
1&1&0&0&0\\
6&6&7&4&5\\
7&4&3&10&5\end{array}\right).

Let
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
u\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
and
v\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{m}}
.
We say that
u

occurs in
v
at position
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
if

v
is large enough, i.e.,
\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and

v(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{p})=u(\mathbf{a})

for all
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that

0\leq a_{i}<n_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq d
.
If
u
occurs in
v
at some position, then we say that
u
is a

d
-dimensional
subword
or
factor
of
v
.

The distinction between tilings and 2-dimensional words is illustrated in the
Figure
5
. Our point of view is to consider Wang tilings
as 2-dimensional words. We
always use the alphabet
\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
to represent a set of Wang tiles of cardinality
n
with some chosen bijection between the two sets.
For Jeandel-Rao tiles, we use the alphabet

\llbracket 0,10\rrbracket

following the order from left to right shown in
Figure
1
.
Depending on the context, we illustrate 2-dimensional words and tilings in this contribution
in one of the three ways depicted in Figure
5

acknowledging that there is a bijection between the representations.

2.6.
d
-dimensional language

A subset
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
is called a
d
-dimensional
language
. The

factorial closure
of a language
L
is

\overline{L}^{Fact}=\{u\in\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}\mid u\text{ is a $d$-dimensional subword of some }v\in L\}.

A language
L
is
factorial
if
\overline{L}^{Fact}=L
.
All languages considered in this contribution are factorial.
Given a tiling
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, the
language

\mathcal{L}(x)
defined by
x
is

\mathcal{L}(x)=\{u\in\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}\mid u\text{ is a $d$-dimensional subword of }x\}.

The
language
of a subshift
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is

\mathcal{L}_{X}=\cup_{x\in X}\mathcal{L}(x)
.
Conversely, given a factorial language
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
we define the subshift

\mathcal{X}_{L}=\{x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mid\mathcal{L}(x)\subseteq L\}.

A
d
-dimensional subword
u\in\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
is
allowed
in a
subshift
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
if
u\in\mathcal{L}_{X}

and it is
forbidden
in
X
if
u\notin\mathcal{L}_{X}
.
A language
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
is
forbidden
in a
subshift

X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
if
L\cap\mathcal{L}_{X}=\varnothing
.

2.7.
d
-dimensional morphisms

In this section, we generalize the definition of
d
-dimensional morphisms

[
CKR10
]
to the case where the domain and codomain are different as for

S
-adic systems
[
BD14
]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be two alphabets.
Let
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
be a factorial language.
A function
\omega:L\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}
is a
d
-dimensional
morphism
if for every

i
with
1\leq i\leq d
,
and every
u,v\in L
such that
u\odot^{i}v\in L
is defined
we have
that the concatenation
\omega(u)\odot^{i}\omega(v)

in direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is defined and

\omega(u\odot^{i}v)=\omega(u)\odot^{i}\omega(v).

Note that the left-hand side of the equation is defined since

u\odot^{i}v
belongs to the domain of
\omega
.
A
d
-dimensional morphism
L\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}
is thus completely defined from the
image of the letters in
\mathcal{A}
, so we sometimes denote
a
d
-dimensional morphism as a rule
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}

when the language
L
is unspecified.

The next lemma can be deduced from the definition.
It says that when
d\geq 2
every
d
-dimensional morphism
defined on the whole set
L=\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
is uniform.
We say that a
d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:L\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}
is
uniform

if there exists a shape
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\omega(a)\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathbf{n}}
for
every letter
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
These are called block-substitutions in
[
Fra18
]
.

Lemma 6
.

If
d\geq 2
, every
d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}
is uniform.

Therefore, to consider non-uniform
d
-dimensional morphisms when
d\geq 2
, we
need to restrict the domain to a strict subset
L\subsetneq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
.
In
[
CKR10
]
and
[
Moz89
, p.144]
, they consider the case
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}

and they restrict the domain of
d
-dimensional morphisms to the language they
generate.

Given a language
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
of
d
-dimensional words and
a
d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:L\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}
, we define the image of the
language
L
under
\omega
as the language

\overline{\omega(L)}^{Fact}=\{u\in\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}\mid u\text{ is a $d$-dimensional subword of }\omega(v)\text{ with }v\in L\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}.

Let
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
be a factorial language
and
\mathcal{X}_{L}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be the subshift generated by
L
.
A
d
-dimensional morphism

\omega:L\to\mathcal{B}^{*^{d}}

can be extended to a
d
-dimensional morphism

\omega:\mathcal{X}_{L}\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

in such a way that the origin of
\omega(x)
is at zero position
in the word
\omega(x_{\mathbf{0}})

for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{L}
.
In general, the closure under the shift of the image of a subshift
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
under
\omega

is the subshift

\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}=\{\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mid\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},x\in X\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}.

Now we show that
d
-dimensional morphisms preserve minimality of subshifts.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\omega:X\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be a
d
-dimensional morphism for some

X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.
If
X
is a minimal subshift, then
\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
is
a minimal subshift.

Proof.

Let
\varnothing\neq Z\subseteq\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
be a closed
shift-invariant subset.
We want to show that
\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}\subseteq Z
.
Let
u\in\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
.
Thus
u=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)
for some
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
x\in X
.
Since
Z\neq\varnothing
, there exists
z\in Z
.
Thus
z=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\omega(x^{\prime})
for some
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
x^{\prime}\in X
.
Since
X
is minimal, there exists a sequence
(\mathbf{k}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathbf{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, such that
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}x^{\prime}
.
For some other sequence
(\mathbf{h}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathbf{h}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have

u=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}x^{\prime}\right)=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma^{\mathbf{h}_{n}}\omega\left(x^{\prime}\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma^{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{h}_{n}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}z.

Since
Z
is closed and shift-invariant, it follows that
u\in Z
.
∎

Suppose now that
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
.
We say that a
d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:L\to\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
, with
L\subset\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
, is

expansive

if for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\min(\textsc{shape}(\omega^{m}(a)))>K
.
We say that
\omega
is
primitive

if there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
for every
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
the letter
b
occurs in
\omega^{m}(a)
.

The definition of prolongable substitutions
[
BR10
, Def.
1.2.18–19]
can be adapted in the case of
d
-dimensional morphisms.
Let
\omega:L\to\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
with
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
be a
d
-dimensional morphism.
Let
s\in\{+1,-1\}^{d}
.
We say that
\omega
is
prolongable on a letter
a\in\mathcal{A}
in the
hyperoctant of sign
s
if the letter
a
appears in the appropriate corner of
its own image
\omega(a)
more precisely at position

p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
where

p_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\quad s_{i}=+1,\\
n_{i}-1&\text{if}\quad s_{i}=-1.\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
is the shape of
\omega(a)
.
If
\omega
is prolongable on letter
a\in\mathcal{A}
in the hyperoctant of
sign
s

and if

\lim_{m\to\infty}\min(\textsc{shape}(\omega^{m}(a)))=\infty
, then

\lim_{m\to\infty}\omega^{m}(a)
is a
d
-dimensional infinite word

s_{0}\mathbb{N}\times\dots\times s_{d-1}\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{A}
.

2.8.
Self-similar subshifts

In this section we consider languages and subshifts defined from substitutions
leading to self-similar structures.
A subshift
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
(resp. a language
L\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
)
is
self-similar

if there exists an expansive

d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
such that

X=\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}

(resp.
L=\overline{\omega(L)}^{Fact}
).

Self-similar languages and subshifts can be constructed by iterative
application of the morphism
\omega
starting with the letters.
The
language

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
defined by an expansive
d
-dimensional
morphism
\omega:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}
is

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\{u\in\mathcal{A}^{*^{d}}\mid u\text{ is a $d$-dimensional subword of }\omega^{n}(a)\text{ for some }a\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

It satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\overline{\omega(\mathcal{L}_{\omega})}^{Fact}

and thus is self-similar.
The
substitutive shift

\mathcal{X}_{\omega}=\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{L}_{\omega}}

defined from the language of
\omega
is a self-similar subshift.
If
\omega
is primitive then
\mathcal{X}_{\omega}
is minimal using standard arguments

[
Que10
, §5.2]
.
Thus
\mathcal{X}_{\omega}
is the smallest nonempty subshift

X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
satisfying
X=\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
.

2.9.
d
-dimensional recognizability and aperiodicity

The definition of recognizability dates back to the work of Host, Quéffelec and
Mossé
[
Mos92
]
. See also
[
ATY17
]
who proposed a
completion of the statement and proof for B. Mossé’s unilateral recognizability
theorem.
The definition introduced below is based on work of Berthé et al.

[
BSTY19
]
on the recognizability in the case
of
S
-adic systems where more than one substitution is involved.

Let
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and

\omega:X\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be a
d
-dimensional morphism.
If
y\in\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
, i.e.,

y=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)
for some
x\in X
and
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, where
\sigma
is
the
d
-dimensional shift map, we say that
(\mathbf{k},x)
is a

\omega
-representation of
y
. We say that it is
centered
if

y_{\mathbf{0}}
lies inside of the image of
x_{\mathbf{0}}
, i.e., if

\mathbf{0}\leq\mathbf{k}<\textsc{shape}(\omega(x_{\mathbf{0}}))
coordinate-wise.
We say that
\omega
is
recognizable in
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

if each
y\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
has at most one centered
\omega
-representation

(\mathbf{k},x)
with
x\in X
.

The next proposition is well-known, see
[
Sol98
,
Mos92
]
, who showed that
recognizability and aperiodicity are equivalent for primitive substitutive
sequences. We state only one direction (the easy one) of the equivalence.
Its proof with the same notations can be found in

[
Lab19a
]
.

Proposition 8
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and

\omega:X\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be an expansive
d
-dimensional morphism.
If
X
is a self-similar subshift such that

\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}=X
and

\omega
is recognizable in
X
, then
X
is aperiodic.

The next lemma is very important for the current contribution.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\omega:X\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be some
d
-dimensional morphism where

X\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a subshift.
If
X
is aperiodic and
\omega
is recognizable in
X
, then

\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
is aperiodic.

Proof.

Let
y\in\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
.
Then, there exist
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
x\in X
such that

(\mathbf{k},x)
is a centered
\omega
-representation of
y
, i.e.,

y=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)
.
Suppose by contradiction that
y
has a nontrivial period

\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\mathbf{0}
.
Since
y=\sigma^{\mathbf{p}}y=\sigma^{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}}\omega(x)
,
we have that

(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},x)
is a
\omega
-representation of
y
.
Since
\omega
is recognizable, this representation is not centered.
Therefore there exists
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\mathbf{0}
such that

y_{\mathbf{0}}
lies in the image of
x_{\mathbf{q}}=(\sigma^{\mathbf{q}}x)_{\mathbf{0}}
.
Therefore there exists
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that

(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\sigma^{\mathbf{q}}x)
is a centered
\omega
-representation of
y
.
Since
\omega
is recognizable, we conclude that

\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
and
x=\sigma^{\mathbf{q}}x
. Then
x\in X
is periodic which is a
contradiction.
∎

Let
X
,
Y
be two subshifts
and
\omega:X\to Y
be a
d
-dimensional morphism.
In general,
\omega(X)
is not closed under the shift which implies that

\omega
is not onto. This motivates the following definition.
If
Y=\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
, then
we say that
\omega

is
onto up to a shift
.

Remark 10
.

We believe the existence of a
d
-dimensional morphism
\omega:X\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

which is recognizable in
X
implies the existence of a

homeomorphism
between
X
and
\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
where
elements of
X
and
\overline{\omega(X)}^{\sigma}
are tilings of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

and the alphabet is replaced by a set of tiles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

(as done in
[
Sol97
]
or see also stone inflations in
[
BG13
]
).
We do not attempt a proof of this here
as we use in this contribution the point of view of symbolic dynamics and tilings
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which makes some notions easier (concatenation, morphisms).
On the other hand, notions like injectivity, surjectivity and
homeomorphisms are less natural as we must use recognizability and expressions
like “onto up to a shift” instead.

Part I
Desubstituting Wang tilings with markers

3.
Markers, desubstitution of Wang shifts and algorithms

In this section, we recall from
[
Lab19a
]
the notion of
markers and the result on the existence of a
2
-dimensional morphism
between two Wang shifts that is recognizable and onto up to a shift.
We also propose new algorithms to find markers and desubstitutions of Wang
shifts.

3.1.
Markers

In what follows we consider a set of Wang tiles as an alphabet and a Wang tiling as

2
-dimensional word. This allows to use the concepts of languages,

2
-dimensional morphisms, recognizability introduced in the preliminaries in
the context of Wang tilings.

Definition 11
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles
and let
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
be its Wang shift.
A nonempty subset
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is called
markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}

if positions of tiles from
M
are nonadjacent columns, that is,
for all tilings
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

there exists
A\subset\mathbb{Z}
such that

w^{-1}(M)=A\times\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{ with }\quad 1\notin A-A.

It is called
markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
if
positions of tiles from
M
are nonadjacent rows, that is,
for all tilings
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

there exists
B\subset\mathbb{Z}
such that

w^{-1}(M)=\mathbb{Z}\times B\quad\text{ with }\quad 1\notin B-B.

Note that it follows from the definition that a subset of markers is a proper
subset of
\mathcal{T}
as the case
M=\mathcal{T}
is impossible.

Figure 6.

This is a copy without colors of the Jeandel-Rao tiling shown in
Figure
2
.
The tiles
M=\{(2,4,2,1),(2,2,2,0)\}\subset\mathcal{T}_{0}
are markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}

and are shown with a blue background. They form complete nonadjacent rows
of tiles in any tilings in
\Omega_{0}
.

Let us illustrate the definition on the set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of Jeandel-Rao tiles.

}\right\}\subset\mathcal{T}_{0}

both have left and right color
2
(blue)
but none of the other nine tiles from
\mathcal{T}_{0}\setminus M
have this color on their
left and right sides (see Figure
1
).
Therefore any valid Wang tiling
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}_{0}
is made of horizontal lines using either tiles from
M
or from
\mathcal{T}_{0}\setminus M

(see Figure
6
).
Moreover, the horizontal lines using tiles from
M
are nonadjacent
simply since top colors of tiles from
M
(2 and 4) do not match their bottom
colors (0 and 1).
Those two conditions implies that
the positions of the tiles in
M
in any tiling are non adjacent complete
rows in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
that is,
M
is a subset of
markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
.
Proving that a subset of tiles is a subset of
markers uses very local observations. It leads to the following criteria.

Lemma 12
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles
and let
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
be its Wang shift.
A nonempty subset
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
if and only if

M\odot^{1}M,\qquad M\odot^{2}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M),\qquad(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{2}M

are forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
A nonempty subset
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
if and only if

M\odot^{2}M,\qquad M\odot^{1}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M),\qquad(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{1}M

are forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
(the proof for direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
is the similar).
For any tiling
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
there exists
B\subset\mathbb{Z}
such that

w^{-1}(M)=\mathbb{Z}\times B
with

1\notin B-B
.
In any tiling
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
this means that the tile to the left and to the right of any tile in
M

also belongs to
M
.
Therefore,
M\odot^{1}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)
and
(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{1}M

are forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Moreover, the fact that

1\notin B-B
implies that

M\odot^{2}M
is forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Conversely, suppose that

M\odot^{2}M
,

M\odot^{1}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)
and

(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{1}M

are forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
The last two conditions implies that
in any tiling
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the tile to the left and to the right
of any tile in
M
also belongs to
M
.
Therefore tiles in
M
appears as complete rows in
w
, that is,

w^{-1}(M)=\mathbb{Z}\times B
for some
B\subset\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
M\odot^{2}M
is forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
, it means that
the rows are nonadjacent, or equivalently,
1\notin B-B
.
We conclude that
M
is a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma
12
provides a way to prove that a subset is a
subset of markers and searching for them. For that, we need the following definition.

Definition 13

(
surrounding of radius
r
).

Let
X=\textsc{SFT}(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
be a shift of finite type for some
finite set
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden patterns. A
2
-dimensional word

u\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}}
,
with
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
admits a
surrounding of radius
r\in\mathbb{N}

if there exists
w\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{n}+2(r,r)}

such that

u
occurs in
w
at position
(r,r)

and

w
contains no occurrences of forbidden patterns from
\mathcal{F}
.

If a word admits a surrounding of radius
r\in\mathbb{N}
, it does not mean
it is in the language of the SFT. But if it admits no surrounding of radius
r

for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
, then for sure it is not in the language of the SFT.
We state the following lemma in the context of Wang tiles.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles and
u\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}
be a rectangular pattern
seen as a
2
-dimensional word with
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. If

u
is allowed in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
, then for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
the word
u
has a
surrounding of radius
r
.

Equivalently the lemma says that
if there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u
has no surrounding of radius
r
,
then
u
is forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
and this is how we use
Lemma
14
to find markers.
We propose Algorithm
1
to compute markers from a Wang
tile set and a chosen surrounding radius to bound the computations.
If the algorithm finds nothing, then maybe there is no markers or maybe
one should try again after increasing the surrounding radius.
We prove in the next lemma that if the output is nonempty, it contains a subset of markers.

Algorithm 1

Find markers.
If no markers are found, one should try increasing the radius
r
.

1:

\mathcal{T}
is a set of Wang tiles;

i\in\{1,2\}
is a direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
;

r\in\mathbb{N}
is some radius.

2:

function

FindMarkers
(
\mathcal{T}
,
i
,
r
)

3:

j\leftarrow 3-i

4:

D_{j}\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}\mid\text{ domino }u\odot^{j}v\text{
admits a surrounding of radius $r$ with tiles in $\mathcal{T}$}\right\}

5:

U\leftarrow\{\{u\}\mid u\in\mathcal{T}\}

\triangleright
Suggestion: use a union-find data structure

6:

for all

(u,v)\in D_{j}

do

7:

Merge the sets containing
u
and
v
in the partition
U
.

8:

D_{i}\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}\mid\text{ domino }u\odot^{i}v\text{
admits a surrounding of radius $r$ with tiles in $\mathcal{T}$}\right\}

9:

return

\{\text{set }M\text{ in the partition }U \mid\left(M\times M\right)\cap D_{i}=\varnothing\}

10:

The output contains zero, one or more subsets of markers
in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

Lemma 15
.

If there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
such that the output of

\textsc{FindMarkers}(\mathcal{T},i,r)

contains a set
M
, then
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
i=2
, the case
i=1
being similar.
The output set
M
is nonempty since is was created from the union of
nonempty sets (see lines 4-6 in Algorithm
1
).
Using Lemma
14
,
lines 3 to 6 implies that

M\odot^{1}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)
and

(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{1}M

are forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
The lines 7 and 8 implies that

M\odot^{2}M
is forbidden in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Then
M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
∎

We believe that if a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
has a subset of markers in the
direction
\mathbf{e}_{i}
then there exists a surrounding radius
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\textsc{FindMarkers}(\mathcal{T},i,r)
outputs this set of markers.
The fact that there is no upper bound for the surrounding radius is related to
the undecidability of the domino problem.
In practice, in the study of Jeandel-Rao tilings, a surrounding radius of 1, 2
or 3 is enough.

3.2.
Desubstitution of Wang shifts

In this section we prove that
if a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
has a subset of marker tiles, then
there exists another set
\mathcal{S}
of Wang tiles and a
nontrivial recognizable
2
-dimensional morphism

\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
that is onto up to a shift.
Thus, every Wang
tiling by
\mathcal{T}
is up to a shift the image under a nontrivial
2
-dimensional
morphism
\omega
of a unique Wang tiling in
\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We will see that the
2
-dimensional morphism is essentially
1
-dimensional.
The result proved here extends the one proved in
[
Lab19a
]
.

Before stating the result, let us see how the two markers in Jeandel-Rao tiles
allows to desubstitute tilings. We know from the previous section that markers
appears as nonadjacent rows in Jeandel-Rao tilings. Therefore the row above
(and below) some row of markers is made of nonmarker tiles. Let us consider the row above.
The idea is to collapse that row onto the row of markers just below and we perform this using
fusion of tiles.
More precisely, the first marker tile has color 4 on top and only one tile has color 4
on the bottom. Therefore, that marker tile is always matched on top with that tile.
Similarly, the second marker tile has color 2 on top and
there are only four tiles
that have color 2 on the bottom. Therefore, the second marker
is always matched on top with one of those 4 tiles.
This means that
one can glue, e.g. with tape, each
marker with one of the possible tiles that can go above of it. Doing so, one
gets five dominoes that may be represented as new Wang tiles with the fusion
operation
\boxminus
introduced in the preliminaries as schematized in the following equation:

Therefore to build some Jeandel-Rao tiling, it is sufficient to build a tiling
with another set
\mathcal{T}_{1}
of Wang tiles obtained from the set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
after removing the
markers and adding the five tiles obtained from the fusion operation.
One may also remove the tile with color
4 on the bottom since it always appear above of a marker tile.
One may recover some Jeandel-Rao tiling by applying a
2
-dimensional morphism
which replaces the five merged tiles by their associated equivalent dominoes,
see Figure
7
.
It turns out
that this decomposition is unique as we prove below.
The creation of the set
\mathcal{T}_{1}
from
\mathcal{T}_{0}
gives the intuition on the
construction of Algorithm
2
which
follows the same recipe and takes any set of Wang tiles with markers as input.

\omega_{0}

Figure 7.
The effect of the
2
-dimensional morphism

\omega_{0}:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{0}
on tilings.

We can now state the small generalization of Theorem 10 from

[
Lab19a
]
. Indeed, while the presence of markers allows to
desubstitute tilings, there is a choice to be made. We may construct the
substitution in such a way that the markers are on the left or
on the right in the image of letters that are dominoes (bottom or top for
marker in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
). We make this distinction in the statement of
the following result.

Theorem 16
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles
and let
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
be its Wang shift.
If there exists a subset

M\subset\mathcal{T}

of markers in the direction

\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}\}
,
then

(1)

there exists
a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}_{R}

and a
2
-dimensional morphism

\omega_{R}:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}_{R}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

such that

\omega_{R}(\mathcal{S}_{R})\subseteq(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\cup\left((\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{i}M\right)

which is recognizable and onto up to a shift and

(2)

there exists a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}_{L}
and a
2
-dimensional morphism

\omega_{L}:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}_{L}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

such that

\omega_{L}(\mathcal{S}_{L})\subseteq(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\cup\left(M\odot^{i}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\right)

which recognizable and onto up to a shift.

There exists a surrounding radius
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\omega_{R}
and

\omega_{L}
are computed using
Algorithm
2
.

Proof.

The existence of
\omega_{R}
was proved in
[
Lab19a
, Theorem
10]
. Its proof follows the line of
Algorithm
2
.

The existence of
\omega_{L}
was not proved in
[
Lab19a
, Theorem
10]
, but it can be deduced by considering the
image of the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
under a reflection by the vertical or
horizontal axis. For example, if
\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{e}_{1}
, then we consider the image
under a reflection by the vertical axis:

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\left\{\raisebox{-8.53581pt}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=(a,b,c,d)\in\mathcal{T}\right\}

and the image
M^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of markers accordingly.
Then
[
Lab19a
, Theorem 10]
provide the existence of
a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
a
2
-dimensional morphism
\omega^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
which is
recognizable, onto up to a shift and
such that

\omega^{\prime}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\subseteq(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\setminus M^{\prime})\cup\left((\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\setminus M^{\prime})\odot^{i}M^{\prime}\right).

By taking the image of
\omega^{\prime}
under a reflection by the vertical axis,
we get the desired
\omega_{L}
and
\mathcal{S}_{L}
.
∎

Algorithm 2

Find a recognizable desubstitution of
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
from markers

1:

\mathcal{T}
is a set of Wang tiles;

M\subset\mathcal{T}
is a subset of markers;

i\in\{1,2\}
is a direction
e_{i}
;

r\in\mathbb{N}
is a surrounding radius;

s\in\{\textsc{left},\textsc{right}\}
determines
whether the image of merged tiles is
in
M\odot^{i}(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)
(markers on the left)
or in
(\mathcal{T}\setminus M)\odot^{i}M
(markers on the right).

2:

function

FindSubstitution
(
\mathcal{T}
,
M
,
i
,
r
,
s
)

3:

D\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}\mid\text{ domino }u\odot^{i}v\text{
admits a surrounding of radius $r$ with tiles in $\mathcal{T}$}\right\}

4:

if

s=\textsc{left}

then

5:

P\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in D\mid u\in M\text{ and }v\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\right\}

6:

K\leftarrow\left\{v\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\mid\text{ there exists }u\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\text{ such that }(u,v)\in D\right\}

7:

else

if

s=\textsc{right}

then

8:

P\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in D\mid u\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\text{ and }v\in M\right\}

9:

K\leftarrow\left\{u\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\mid\text{ there exists }v\in\mathcal{T}\setminus M\text{ such that }(u,v)\in D\right\}

10:

K\leftarrow\textsc{Sort}(K)
,
P\leftarrow\textsc{Sort}(P)

\triangleright
lexicographically on the indices of tiles

11:

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow K\cup\{u\boxslash^{i}v\mid(u,v)\in P\}

\triangleright
defines uniquely indices of tiles in
\mathcal{S}
from
0
to

|\mathcal{S}|-1
.

12:

return

\mathcal{S}
,
\omega:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}:\begin{cases}u\boxslash^{i}v\quad\mapsto\quad u\odot^{i}v&\text{ if }(u,v)\in P\\
u\quad\mapsto\quad u&\text{ if }u\in K.\end{cases}

13:

\mathcal{S}
is a set of Wang tiles;

\omega:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is
recognizable and onto up to a shift.

In the definition of
\omega

in Algorithm
2
,
given two Wang tiles
u
and
v
such that

u\boxslash^{i}v
is defined for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the map

u\boxslash^{i}v\quad\mapsto\quad u\odot^{i}v

can be seen as a decomposition of Wang tiles:
Z
BD
X
AC
\mapsto
Y
B
X
A
Z
D
Y
C

or

YZ
D
XW
A
\mapsto
Y
B
X
A
Z
D
W
B

whether
i=1
or
i=2
.
The reader may wonder how the substitution decides the color
Y
(color
B
if

i=2
) from its input tiles. The answer is that
Algorithm
2
is performing
a desubstitution. Therefore the two tiles sharing the vertical side with
letter
Y
are known from the start and the algorithm just creates a new tile

(Z,BD,X,AC)
and claims that it will always get replaced by the two tiles with
shared edge with color
Y
.

Remark 17
.

In Algorithm
2
, the set of tiles

\mathcal{S}
can be computed with the help of transducers since
the fusion operation
\boxslash^{i}
on Wang tiles can be seen as the composition
of transitions (see Equation (
5
)).

4.
Desubstitution from
\Omega_{0}
to
\Omega_{4}
:

\Omega_{0}\xleftarrow{\omega_{0}}\Omega_{1}\xleftarrow{\omega_{1}}\Omega_{2}\xleftarrow{\omega_{2}}\Omega_{3}\xleftarrow{\omega_{3}^{\prime}}\Omega_{4}

When Algorithm
1
returns a nonempty list of sets of
markers, then we may use Algorithm
2

to find the substitution from the markers found. This allows to desubstitute
uniquely tilings in
\Omega_{0}
into tilings in
\Omega_{4}
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
\Omega_{0}
be the Jeandel-Rao Wang shift.
There exist sets of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}
,
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{4}

together with their associated Wang shifts

\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
,
\Omega_{3}
and
\Omega_{4}

and there exists a sequence of recognizable
2
-dimensional morphisms:

\Omega_{0}\xleftarrow{\omega_{0}}\Omega_{1}\xleftarrow{\omega_{1}}\Omega_{2}\xleftarrow{\omega_{2}}\Omega_{3}\xleftarrow{\omega_{3}^{\prime}}\Omega_{4}

that are onto up to a shift, i.e.,

\overline{\omega_{i}(\Omega_{i+1})}^{\sigma}=\Omega_{i}

for each
i\in\{0,1,2\}
and

\overline{\omega_{3}^{\prime}(\Omega_{4})}^{\sigma}=\Omega_{3}
.

Proof.

The proof is done by executing the function

\textsc{FindMarkers}(
)
followed by

\textsc{FindSubstitution}(
)
and repeating this process four times in total.
Each time
\textsc{FindMarkers}(
)
finds at least one subset of markers using a surrounding radius
of size at most 3.
Thus using Theorem
16
,
we may find a desubstitution of tilings.

The proof is done in SageMath
[
Sag19
]

using
slabbe
optional package

[
Lab19b
]
.
First we define the Jeandel-Rao set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{0}
:

sage: from slabbe import WangTileSet
sage: tiles = [(2,4,2,1), (2,2,2,0), (1,1,3,1), (1,2,3,2), (3,1,3,3), (0,1,3,1),
....: (0,0,0,1), (3,1,0,2), (0,2,1,2), (1,2,1,4), (3,3,1,2)]
sage: tiles = [[str(a) for a in tile] for tile in tiles]
sage: T0 = WangTileSet(tiles)

\mathcal{T}_{0}=\left\{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\includegraphics[]{article2_T0_tiles.pdf}}\right\}

We use Knuth’s dancing links algorithm
[
Knu00
]
because it is faster at this task than
MILP or SAT solvers.
We desubstitute
\mathcal{T}_{0}
:

sage: T0.find_markers(i=2, radius=1, solver="dancing_links")
[[0, 1]]
sage: M0 = [0,1]
sage: T1,omega0 = T0.find_substitution(M=M0, i=2, side="left",
....: radius=1, solver="dancing_links")

and we obtain:

\omega_{0}:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{0}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllllll}0\mapsto\left(2\right),&1\mapsto\left(3\right),&2\mapsto\left(4\right),&3\mapsto\left(5\right),&4\mapsto\left(6\right),&5\mapsto\left(7\right),&6\mapsto\left(8\right),\\

0\end{array}\right),&9\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}3\\
1\end{array}\right),&10\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}7\\
1\end{array}\right),&11\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}8\\
1\end{array}\right),&12\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}10\\
1\end{array}\right).\end{array}\right.

\mathcal{T}_{1}=\left\{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\includegraphics[]{article2_T1_tiles.pdf}}\right\}

The above computation of

confirms the observations we made

.

and we obtain:

∎

The set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
contains 30 tiles. We show in
the next section that it contains 2 useless tiles,
i.e., tiles that never appear in any tiling of
\Omega_{4}
,
so that we can remove those two tiles from
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
.

5.
Removing two useless tiles from
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
to get
\mathcal{T}_{4}

In this section, we state some observations on what cannot be done
with the Jeandel-Rao set of tiles. This allows to gain some initial local
intuitions on what is allowed and what is forbidden in Jeandel-Rao tilings but
also illustrates that not everything can be decided by using only a small
neighborhood.

5.1.
Some forbidden patterns in Jeandel-Rao tilings

The next lemma shows easily by hand that some patterns are forbidden in

\Omega_{0}
. The first two patterns were already proven impossible in

[
JR15
, Proposition 1]
.

Lemma 19
.

No tiling of the plane with Jeandel-Rao set of tiles contains
any of the patterns shown in Figure
8
.

Figure 8.
Those patterns are forbidden in any tiling of the plane using
Jeandel-Rao set of tiles. The first two were proved impossible in Proposition 1
of in
[
JR15
]
.

Proof.

There is no way to find three tiles to put above (resp. below) the first
(resp. second) pattern. Indeed, the two on the side are forced, but no
tile can fit in the middle:

,

.

The only way to put three tiles above the third pattern is to use the first
pattern which we just have shown is forbidden:

.

The only way to put four tiles to the left and to the right of the
fourth pattern is shown below and we remark the third forbidden pattern appears
on the top row:

.

There is a unique way to tile the
1\times 4
rectangle to the left of the
fifth considered pattern:

We observe that the row on the bottom is forbidden.
∎

5.2.
Jeandel-Rao transducer representation of
\mathcal{T}_{4}

In
[
JR15
]
, the set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of 11 Wang
tiles is represented as a transducer made of two connected components
T_{0}

and
T_{1}
, see Figure
4
.
They observed that the strongly connected
components of the product of transducers
T_{1}T_{1}
,
T_{1}T_{0}T_{1}
,

T_{1}T_{0}T_{0}T_{1}
and
T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}
are empty.
The emptiness of the products imply that the only possible tilings in

\Omega_{0}
are made of horizontal strips of height 4 or 5 generated by the
products
T_{a}=T_{1}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}
and
T_{b}=T_{1}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}

shown in Figure
9
.

Such tilings indeed exist as proved by
[
JR15
]
.
Jeandel and Rao proved aperiodicity of
\mathcal{T}_{0}
by proving that
the sequence of horizontal strips of height
5
and of height

4
coded respectively by the transducers
T_{a}
and
T_{b}

is exactly the set of factors of the Fibonacci word, i.e., the fixed point of
the morphism
a\mapsto ab,b\mapsto a
(see also
Proposition
29
).

As a consequence, every tiling in
\Omega_{0}
can be desubstituted uniquely by the 31
patterns of rectangular shape
1\times 4
or
1\times 5
associated to each
of the transitions of the two transducers shown at
Figure
9
.
The reader may compare these 31 transitions with the set
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
of 30 Wang
tiles computed in the previous section and see that the transition

2030\xrightarrow{0|0}2310
does not correspond to a tile in
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
.

T_{a}=T_{1}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}T_{0}
(below) and

(above) after deletion of sink and source
transitions. This is a reproduction of Figure 7 (b) page 18 of
Jeandel-Rao preprint.
The edges

2030\xrightarrow{0|0}2310

and

21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103

are dashed because we show that the associated patterns are forbidden in
any tiling of the plane (Lemma
19
and
Proposition
20
).
Each solid transition corresponds to a Wang tile in the set
\mathcal{T}_{4}
.

Figure 10.

From left to right:
the pattern in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
;
the tile
associated to the transition
2030\xrightarrow{0|0}2310

which does not appear in
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
;
the pattern in
\mathcal{T}_{0}

and the two tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}

associated to the consecutive transitions

21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103
.
All these patterns are forbidden in
\Omega_{0}
and in
\Omega_{4}
.
The proof for the former transition is easy and is done in
Lemma
19
.
The proof for the latter is impossible without a computer.

In this section we show that only 28 of the 31 transitions shown in
Figure
9
correspond to patterns that
appear in
\Omega_{0}
.
The patterns in

\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{4}
associated to the three transitions that can be
removed are shown in Figure
10
.
The proof that the pattern associated to the transition
2030\xrightarrow{0|0}2310
does not appear in
\Omega_{0}
is easy and is done in
Lemma
19
.
Another independent proof of this is
Proposition
18
since the tile
(2310,0,2030,0)

associated to the transition
2030\xrightarrow{0|0}2310

does not belong to
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
.

The proof that the other two transitions are useless is much more difficult
as some large valid surroundings of the pattern
associated to
21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103

can be found (see Figure
11
), so we have to go far until we
reach a forbidden pattern.
Moreover Proposition
18

fails to prove it since
the two tiles associated to the transitions

21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103

belong to
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
.

Figure 11.
A surrounding of height 9 and width 14 with tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}
around
the pattern
21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103
.

5.3.
Removing two tiles from
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}

The first proof of this result was provided by Michaël Rao using the transducer
approach during a visit of him at LaBRI, Bordeaux France (personal
communication, November 2017). We provide here an independent proof
also done by computer exploration with SageMath
[
Sag19
]
.

Proposition 20
.

(Michaël Rao, 2017)
No tiling of the plane with Jeandel-Rao set of tiles contains the
pattern
21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103
.

Proof.

To show that the pattern
23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103
is forbidden is
enough as it must occur if and only if the other pattern

21330\xrightarrow{0|0}23310
occurs. We show that there exists no
rectangle of width 71 and height 9 tiled with the 30 tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}

with the tile
23310\xrightarrow{0|1}21103
in the center of that rectangle.

We do the proof
in SageMath
[
Sag19
]
with the open source optional package

slabbe

[
Lab19b
]
developed by the author.
In that package,
a Wang tiling problem is reduced to
mixed integer linear program (MILP),
boolean satisfiability problem (SAT)
or exact cover problem (solved with Knuth’s dancing links algorithm).

Below is the code to construct the problem. We
construct the solver searching for
a tiling of the rectangle of size
71\times 9

tiled with the tiles from
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}

with the tile
(21103,1,23310,0)

in the center of that rectangle,
that is, at position
(35,4)
:

sage: T4p[24]
(’21103’, ’1’, ’23310’, ’0’)
sage: S = T4p.solver(width=71, height=9, preassigned_tiles={(35,4):24})

Using a reduction of that problem to SAT
using 19170 variables and 1078659 clauses,
the Glucose SAT solver
[
AS19
]
says there is no solution in less than 4 seconds:

sage: %time S.has_solution(solver="glucose")
CPU times: user 2.02 s, sys: 104 ms, total: 2.12 s
Wall time: 3.93 s
False

Using a reduction of the same problem to a MILP instance,
using 19170 variables and 1838 constraints,
the linear program is shown to have no solution using

Gurobi
solver
[
GO18
]

in about 45 seconds on a normal 2018 desktop computer with 8 available
cpus:

sage: %time S.has_solution(solver="Gurobi")

∎

.

There exists a one-to-one map
\iota:\mathcal{T}_{4}\to\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\prime}

such that its extension to the Wang shift
\Omega_{4}

is the identity.
The morphism
\omega_{3}:\Omega_{4}\to\Omega_{3}
defined as

\omega_{3}=\omega_{3}^{\prime}\circ\iota

is recognizable and onto up to a shift.

Both
\mathcal{T}_{4}
and
\iota:\Omega_{4}\to\Omega_{4}
are
shown below.

The next natural step would be to find markers
M\subset\mathcal{T}_{4}
but
we face a problem:
\mathcal{T}_{4}
has no markers.
Even worse: there are two problems. Each of these two distinct reasons is dealt
in the next two sections.

Part II
Dealing with the troubles

6.
Adding decorations :
\Omega_{4}\xleftarrow{\jmath}\Omega_{5}

As mentioned, the set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{4}
has no markers.
It has no markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
for a reason that is dealt in the next section.
It has no markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
because
there are tilings in
\Omega_{4}
that have a horizontal biinfinite line
consisting of color 0 only (there are also some other tilings that have a
horizontal biinfinite line of 1’s only).
We call such a line of a constant color a
fault line
.
The upper half-plane above a horizontal fault line can be translated
to the left or to the right keeping the validity of the tiling. The validity of this
sliding operation prevents the existence of markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
in
\mathcal{T}_{4}
as
it naturally breaks the column-structure imposed by such marker tiles.

Horizontal fault lines also happens for Jeandel-Rao tilings themselves.
See Figure
2
which illustrates a tiling in
\Omega_{0}
containing a fault line of 0’s.
This implies that the set
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of Jeandel-Rao tiles also has no markers in
the direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
although it has markers in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
.
The reader may observe in Figure
2
that sliding
the upper half-plane above the horizontal fault line of 0’s breaks the structure of supertiles.
Such tilings are still valid Wang tilings, they belong to the Jeandel-Rao Wang shift
but they can not be desubstituted further since they do not belong to
X_{0}
(see Theorem
1
). Moreover, we believe that
\Omega_{0}\setminus X_{0}

contains nothing else than those obtained by sliding along the fault line
(see Conjecture
37
).
This is also why the Jeandel-Rao Wang
shift is not minimal and this is developed later in Part
IV
of this contribution.

Fault lines happen also with Robinson tilings and this is the
reason why the Robinson tilings are not minimal. In
[
GJS12
]
, the
authors add decorations on Robinson tiles to avoid fault lines and they described
the unique minimal subshift of Robinson tilings.

In this section, to avoid fault lines in
\Omega_{4}
, we add decorations on the
bottom and top edges of tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{4}
. We replace some 0’s by 6’s and some
1’s by 5’s. More precisely, we define a new set
\mathcal{T}_{5}
of 29 Wang tiles as

\mathcal{T}_{5}=\left\{\raisebox{-42.67912pt}{\includegraphics[]{article2_T5_tiles.pdf}}\right\}

together with its Wang shift
\Omega_{5}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{5}}
.
We define the following projection of colors:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\jmath:\{0,1,5,6\}&\to&\{0,1\}\\
x&\mapsto&\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }x\in\{0,6\},\\
1&\text{ if }x\in\{1,5\}.\end{cases}\end{array}

When
\jmath
is applied to the bottom and top colors of tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{5}
,
we obtain another map (that we also denote by
\jmath
)

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\jmath:&\mathcal{T}_{5}&\to&\mathcal{T}_{4}\\

which maps the tiles

The map
\jmath
