But
\jmath
defines a one-to-one
2
-dimensional morphism on tilings of

\Omega_{5}
given by

\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllllll}0\mapsto\left(0\right),&1\mapsto\left(1\right),&2\mapsto\left(2\right),&3\mapsto\left(3\right),&4\mapsto\left(4\right),&5\mapsto\left(5\right),&6\mapsto\left(6\right),\\
7\mapsto\left(7\right),&8\mapsto\left(8\right),&9\mapsto\left(9\right),&10\mapsto\left(10\right),&11\mapsto\left(11\right),&12\mapsto\left(12\right),&13\mapsto\left(13\right),\\
14\mapsto\left(14\right),&15\mapsto\left(15\right),&16\mapsto\left(16\right),&17\mapsto\left(17\right),&18\mapsto\left(18\right),&19\mapsto\left(19\right),&20\mapsto\left(20\right),\\
21\mapsto\left(21\right),&22\mapsto\left(22\right),&23\mapsto\left(22\right),&24\mapsto\left(23\right),&25\mapsto\left(24\right),&26\mapsto\left(25\right),&27\mapsto\left(26\right),\\
28\mapsto\left(27\right).\end{array}\right.

Proposition 22
.

The
2
-dimensional morphism
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is an embedding
and is a topological conjugacy onto its image
X_{4}=\jmath(\Omega_{5})
.

We recall that an embedding commutes with the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-actions, i.e., the shift
maps
\sigma
.

Proof.

First we prove that the map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is well-defined.
Indeed, we check that for each tile
u\in\mathcal{T}_{5}
, we have
\jmath(u)\in\mathcal{T}_{4}
.
Moreover, if tiles
u,v\in\mathcal{T}_{5}
can be adjacent in a tiling in
\Omega_{5}
,
then
\jmath(u)
,
\jmath(v)
can be adjacent in a tiling of
\Omega_{4}
.
Therefore, if
x\in\Omega_{5}
, then
\jmath(x)\in\Omega_{4}
which proves that
the map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is well-defined.

Now we prove that the map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is one-to-one.
Suppose that
x,y\in\Omega_{5}
are such that
\jmath(x)=\jmath(y)
.
Let
z\in\Omega_{4}
be the tiling such that
z=\jmath(x)=\jmath(y)
.
Let
P\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the positions of tiles in
z
that have more than one
preimage under
\jmath
.
Notice that the only two tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{5}
that are mapped to the same tile
in
\mathcal{T}_{4}
are the ones given at Equation (
6
).
So we have:

P=z^{-1}\left(\{t\in\mathcal{T}_{4}\mid\#\jmath^{-1}(t)>1\}\right)=z^{-1}\left(\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{
\hbox to34.54pt{\vbox to34.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 47.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 47.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.24 47.24 L 47.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.24 47.24 L 0 47.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{1.0}{-1.0}{0.0}{29.89244pt}{6.44646pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.94658pt}{24.7369pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{1.0}{-1.0}{0.0}{9.4063pt}{6.44646pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.94658pt}{4.25075pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\;\right).

By definition, we have
x_{p}=y_{p}
for every
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus P
.
Let
p\in P
and suppose by contradiction that
x_{p}\neq y_{p}
.
From Equation (
6
), this means that the bottom
colors of
x_{p}
and
y_{p}
are distinct:

\textsc{bottom}(x_{p})\neq\textsc{bottom}(y_{p}).

This also means that the top colors of the tile just below are:

\{\textsc{top}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}),\textsc{top}(y_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}})\}=\{\textsc{bottom}(x_{p}),\textsc{bottom}(y_{p})\}=\{1,5\}.

Therefore we have that
p-\mathbf{e}_{2}\notin P
or otherwise

\{\textsc{top}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}),\textsc{top}(y_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}})\}=\{0\}
.
This means that
x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}=y_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}
.
Therefore the top colors of
x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}=y_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}
are the same which
implies that the bottom colors of
x_{p}
and
y_{p}
are the same

\textsc{bottom}(x_{p})=\textsc{top}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}})=\textsc{top}(y_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}})=\textsc{bottom}(y_{p})

which is a contradiction. This proves that
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is
one-to-one.

We prove that
\jmath
is continuous.
If
x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega_{5}
then

\delta(\jmath(x),\jmath(x^{\prime}))=\delta(x,x^{\prime}).

Thus if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of tilings
x_{n}\in\Omega_{5}
such
that
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}
exists, then

\jmath(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\jmath(x_{n})
and
\jmath
is continuous.
∎

The map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
is not onto but we believe it is
\mu
-almost
the case where
\mu
is any shift invariant probability measure on
\Omega_{4}
.
In Section
11

we provide an example of a tiling
x\in\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}

and we explain why we believe that

\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}
has measure 0.
The code to construct the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{5}
introduced in this section is below:

sage: tiles5 = [(2113, 5, 2130, 1), (2130, 1, 2103, 5), (2133, 1, 2113, 1),
....: (2113, 5, 2330, 0), (2130, 6, 2300, 0), (2103, 5, 2310, 0),
....: (2310, 1, 2033, 6), (2300, 1, 2033, 6), (2300, 0, 2030, 6),
....: (2030, 1, 2103, 0), (2033, 1, 2113, 0), (2330, 1, 2133, 6),
....: (2330, 0, 2130, 6), (21113, 5, 21330, 1), (21130, 6, 21300, 1),
....: (21103, 5, 21310, 1), (21310, 1, 21033, 5), (21310, 0, 21030, 5),
....: (21300, 1, 21033, 5), (21300, 0, 21030, 5), (21030, 1, 21103, 1),
....: (21033, 1, 21113, 1), (21330, 0, 21130, 1), (21330, 0, 21130, 5),
....: (21130, 6, 23300, 0), (21030, 6, 23100, 0), (23100, 0, 20330, 6),
....: (20330, 0, 21130, 0), (23300, 0, 21330, 6)]
sage: T5 = WangTileSet([[str(a) for a in tile] for tile in tiles5])

We finish this section by a proof that the image of
\jmath
is subshift of
finite type. This result will be helpful in a future work on
X_{0}
but its
proof is more easily done here.

Lemma 23
.

X_{0}
,
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
,
X_{3}
and
X_{4}
are subshifts of finite type.

Proof.

We want to show that
X_{4}=\textsc{SFT}(G)
where
G
is the
following set of forbidden dominoes

G=\left(\left(\mathcal{T}_{4}\odot^{1}\mathcal{T}_{4}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{T}_{4}\odot^{2}\mathcal{T}_{4}\right)\right)\setminus\jmath(D)

and
D
is the set of dominoes of tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{5}
that allows some
surrounding:

D=\left\{u\odot^{i}v\in\mathcal{T}_{5}^{*^{2}}\mid i\in\{1,2\}\text{ and }u\odot^{i}v\text{
admits a surrounding of radius $3$ with tiles in $\mathcal{T}_{5}$}\right\}.

The set
D
contains 37 horizontal dominoes and 75 vertical dominoes as
shown in the computation below.

sage: D_horizontal = T5.dominoes_with_surrounding(i=1, radius=3, solver="dancing_links")
sage: len(D_horizontal)
37
sage: D_vertical = T5.dominoes_with_surrounding(i=2, radius=3, solver="dancing_links")
sage: len(D_vertical)
75

We show that
\textsc{SFT}(G)\supseteq X_{4}
.
Since
X_{4}=\jmath(\Omega_{5})
, let
x\in\Omega_{5}
.
Let
a\odot^{i}b
be a domino appearing in
\jmath(x)
.
There exists some domino
u\odot^{i}v
appearing in
x

such that
a\odot^{i}b=\jmath(u\odot^{i}v)
.
Thus
u\odot^{i}v\in D
and
a\odot^{i}b=\jmath(u\odot^{i}v)\in\jmath(D)
.
Therefore
a\odot^{i}b\notin G
.
Thus
\jmath(x)\in\textsc{SFT}(G)
.

We show that
\textsc{SFT}(G)\subseteq X_{4}
.
Let us identify the tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{4}
with their indices
in
\llbracket 0,27\rrbracket

and the tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{5}
with their indices
in
\llbracket 0,28\rrbracket
.
Recall that the map
\jmath
is one-to-one everywhere on
\mathcal{T}_{5}
except
tiles 22 and 23 that are mapped to the tile 22.
Note that among the vertical dominoes in
D
, the only one where the top tile
is 22 or 23 are

7\odot^{2}22
,

18\odot^{2}22
,

0\odot^{2}23
,

3\odot^{2}23
and

13\odot^{2}23
:

sage: sorted((u,v) for (u,v) in D_vertical if v in [22,23])
[(0, 23), (3, 23), (7, 22), (13, 23), (18, 22)]

Let
x\in\textsc{SFT}(G)\subset\llbracket 0,27\rrbracket^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
.
We want to construct
y\in\Omega_{5}
such that
x=\jmath(y)
.
Let
y\in\mathcal{T}_{5}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}=\llbracket 0,28\rrbracket^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}

such that for every
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we have

y_{p}=\begin{cases}\jmath^{-1}(x_{p})&\text{if}\quad x_{p}\neq 22,\\
22&\text{if}\quad x_{p}=22\text{ and }x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}\in\{7,18\},\\
23&\text{if}\quad x_{p}=22\text{ and }x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{2}}\in\{0,3,13\}.\end{cases}

By definition, we have
x=\jmath(y)
.
Suppose by contradiction that
y\notin\Omega_{5}
.
This means that
y
contains a domino
a\odot^{i}b

which is not valid, in particular
a\odot^{i}b\notin D
.
Then
\jmath(a\odot^{i}b)
is a domino which occurs in
x\in\textsc{SFT}(G)
.
Thus
\jmath(a\odot^{i}b)\notin G
which means

\jmath(a\odot^{i}b)\in\jmath(D)
.
Therefore there exists
c\odot^{i}d\in D
such that

\jmath(a\odot^{i}b)=\jmath(c\odot^{i}d)
.
The equality
a\odot^{i}b=c\odot^{i}d
leads to a contradiction.
Then
\{a,c\}=\{22,23\}
or
\{b,d\}=\{22,23\}
.
The fact that
c\odot^{2}d\in D
and
a\odot^{2}b\notin D

and since only the bottom edge of tiles
22
and
23
in
\mathcal{T}_{5}
differ
implies that
i=2
,
\{b,d\}=\{22,23\}
.
Tiles
\{22,23\}
are not adjacent in
y
since tile
22
is not
adjacent in
x
. It implies that
\{a,c\}\cap\{22,23\}=\varnothing
.
Thus
\jmath(a)=\jmath(c)
implies that
a=c
.
Since
a\odot^{2}b
is not valid,
we must have
b=23
and
d=22
.
We reach a contradiction because
all occurrences of
a\odot^{2}23
in
y

are valid since
a\in\jmath^{-1}(\{0,3,13\})=\{0,3,13\}
.

From the recognizability of
\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}
,
it follows that
X_{0}=\textsc{SFT}(\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\omega_{3}(G)\cup H)

where

H=\left(\left(\mathcal{T}_{0}\odot^{1}\mathcal{T}_{0}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{T}_{0}\odot^{2}\mathcal{T}_{0}\right)\right)\setminus\left\{u\odot^{i}v\mid u,v\in\mathcal{T}_{0},u\odot^{i}v\text{ is valid and }i\in\{1,2\}\right\}.

Thus
X_{0}
is a subshift of finite type and similarly for intermediate

X_{1}
,
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
.
∎

7.
A shear conjugacy:
\Omega_{5}\xleftarrow{\eta}\Omega_{6}

In the previous section, we added decorations to avoid horizontal fault lines
but this is not enough.
There are still no subset of
markers in
\mathcal{T}_{5}
neither in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
nor in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{2}
.
But the tilings in
\Omega_{5}
have a particular property
illustrated in Figure
12
. On each line of
slope 1 in a tiling in
\Omega_{5}
, we see tiles from a subset
M\subset\mathcal{T}_{5}
.
To deal with this case, we need to adapt the approach.

Figure 12.

In a tiling in
\Omega_{5}
, all tiles appear in any chosen vertical
column, so one can not find markers in direction
\mathbf{e}_{1}
nor
\mathbf{e}_{2}
to
desubstitute those tilings. But the tiles appearing on a line of slope
1 belong to either of the following three sets:

\{1,6,7,8,11,12,16,17,18,19,23,26,28\}
(yellow),

\{0,3,4,5,13,14,15,24,25\}
(green),

\{2,9,10,20,21,22,27\}
(blue).
These are
kind of diagonal
markers.

One possibility is to generalize the notion of markers to directions other than

\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}
including markers appearing on lines of slopes
1
,
-1

and possibly other rational slopes.
A bad consequence of this option is that we need to
adapt all subsequent steps including Theorem
16
and
Algorithm
2
.
Their modification is possible but the resulting new algorithms will be more
complicated as they need to compute patches of size at least
2\times 2

with their surroundings. If possible, we want to avoid to do that.
Instead, we change bijectively the set of tiles
\mathcal{T}_{5}
so that the tilings
with the new tiles
\mathcal{T}_{6}
are the image under the application of a shear matrix
on the tilings with the original tiles.

In this section, we introduce an operation on any set of Wang tiles which has
the effect of the application of a shear matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

on the associated tilings. Let

\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles and

\textsc{Dominoes}^{i}(\mathcal{T})=\{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}\mid\text{ the pattern }s\odot^{i}t\text{ appears in some tilings of }\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\}

be the set of allowed dominoes in the direction
i\in\{1,2\}
.
We define the map
\theta
as the fusion of some hidden flat tile that appears
on top of two adjacent tiles (see
Figure
13
):

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\theta:&\textsc{Dominoes}^{1}(\mathcal{T})&\to&\mathcal{S}\\

Figure 13.
A step by step visual explanation of the map

\theta:\textsc{Dominoes}^{1}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{S}
. A flat tile

(B,B,A,A)
on top of two adjacent tiles is unfolded and merged
with the left one with the fusion operation
\boxslash^{2}
.
On tilings this operation is a shear conjugacy

\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
corresponding to the
application of a shear transformation.

The map
\theta
extends to tilings
as a map
\theta:\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
.
For any
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\theta(x):&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\to&\mathcal{S}\\
&p&\mapsto&\theta(x_{Mp},x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})\end{array}

where
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The matrix
M
is essential in the definition of
\theta
because it assures
that
\theta(x)
is a valid tiling of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which is proved in the next lemma.

The map
\eta
extends to tilings

\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

as follows. For any
y\in\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
,

(7)

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\eta(y):&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\to&\mathcal{T}\\
&p&\mapsto&\eta(y_{M^{-1}p})\end{array}

where
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
We show in the next lemma that
\eta
is well-defined and is the inverse of
\theta
.

Lemma 24
.

The map
\theta:\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
is a shear conjugacy
satisfying

\theta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}=\sigma^{M^{-1}\mathbf{n}}\circ\theta

with

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof.

First we show that
\theta
is well-defined.
Let
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
and
y=\theta(x)
.
Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We prove that colors on vertical edges match:

\displaystyle\textsc{right}(y_{p})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(x_{Mp})\cdot\textsc{top}(x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\textsc{left}(x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})\cdot\textsc{top}(x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\textsc{left}(x_{M(p+\mathbf{e}_{1})})\cdot\textsc{top}(x_{M(p+\mathbf{e}_{1})})=\textsc{left}(y_{p+\mathbf{e}_{1}}).

We prove that colors on horizontal edges match:

\textsc{top}(y_{p})=\textsc{top}(x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})=\textsc{bottom}(x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}})=\textsc{bottom}(x_{M(p+\mathbf{e}_{2})})=\textsc{bottom}(y_{p+\mathbf{e}_{2}}).

We prove that
\theta
is one-to-one. Suppose that
x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
are two
tilings such that
x\neq x^{\prime}
. Then there exists
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

x_{p}\neq x^{\prime}_{p}
. Let
y=\theta(x)
and
y^{\prime}=\theta(x^{\prime})
.
Since
\theta:(\textsc{Dominoes}^{1}(\mathcal{T}))\to\mathcal{S}
is one-to-one on the first coordinate,
we have

y_{M^{-1}p}=\theta(x_{p},x_{p+\mathbf{e}_{1}})\neq\theta(x^{\prime}_{p},x^{\prime}_{p+\mathbf{e}_{1}})=y^{\prime}_{M^{-1}p}.

Therefore
\theta(x)\neq\theta(x^{\prime})
.

We prove that the map
\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is well-defined.
To achieve this, we prove that colors on vertical edges match.
Let
y\in\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
,
x=\eta(y)
and
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We have

\displaystyle\textsc{left}(x_{p})\cdot\textsc{top}(x_{p})

\displaystyle=\textsc{left}(y_{M^{-1}p})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(y_{M^{-1}p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(y_{M^{-1}(p-\mathbf{e}_{1})})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})\cdot\textsc{top}(y_{M^{-1}(p-\mathbf{e}_{1})})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})\cdot\textsc{bottom}(y_{M^{-1}(p-\mathbf{e}_{1})+\mathbf{e}_{2}})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})\cdot\textsc{bottom}(y_{M^{-1}(p+\mathbf{e}_{2})})

\displaystyle=\textsc{right}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})\cdot\textsc{bottom}(x_{p+\mathbf{e}_{2}})

from which we deduce

\displaystyle\textsc{left}(x_{p})=\textsc{right}(x_{p-\mathbf{e}_{1}})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\textsc{top}(x_{p})=\textsc{bottom}(x_{p+\mathbf{e}_{2}}).

Thus
x=\eta(y)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is a valid Wang tiling.

We now prove that
\theta
is onto. Let
y\in\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}

and take
x=\eta(y)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
We have
\theta(x)=y
since

\theta(x)_{p}=\theta(x_{Mp},x_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})=\theta(\eta(y_{M^{-1}Mp}),y_{M^{-1}(Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1})})=\theta(\eta(y_{p}),\eta(y_{p+\mathbf{e}_{1}}))=y_{p}.

Therefore
\theta
is onto and
\eta
is the inverse of
\theta
.

We prove that
\theta
and
\eta
are continuous.
If
x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

\delta(\theta(x),\theta(x^{\prime}))<\sqrt{\delta(x,x^{\prime})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\delta(\eta(y),\eta(y^{\prime}))<\sqrt{\delta(y,y^{\prime})}.

Thus if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of tilings
x_{n}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
such
that
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}
exists, then

\theta(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta(x_{n})
and
\theta
is continuous.
Similarly,
\eta
is continuous.

We prove that
\theta
is a shear conjugacy.
If
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\left[\sigma^{M^{-1}\mathbf{n}}\circ\theta(x)\right](p)

\displaystyle=\left[\theta(x)\right](p+M^{-1}\mathbf{n})=\theta(x_{M(p+M^{-1}\mathbf{n})},x_{M(p+M^{-1}\mathbf{n})+\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\theta(x_{Mp+\mathbf{n}},x_{Mp+\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{1}})=\theta((\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}x)_{Mp},(\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}x)_{Mp+\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\left[\theta(\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}x)\right](p)=\left[\theta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)\right](p).

Thus the map
\theta:\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}
is a shear conjugacy
satisfying

\theta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}=\sigma^{M^{-1}\mathbf{n}}\circ\theta

with

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
∎

Corollary 25
.

The map
\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is a shear conjugacy
satisfying

\eta\circ\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}=\sigma^{M\mathbf{n}}\circ\eta

with

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Of course, as a consequence, the map
\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is also
recognizable and onto up to a shift.
The algorithm to compute the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{S}
and the shear conjugacy

\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

that shears tiling by the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Shear Wang tilings by the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

1:

\mathcal{T}
is a set of Wang tiles;

r\in\mathbb{N}
is a surrounding radius.

2:

function

Shear
(
\mathcal{T}
,
r
)

3:

D_{1}\leftarrow\left\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}\mid\text{ domino }u\odot^{1}v\text{
admits a surrounding of radius $r$ with tiles in $\mathcal{T}$}\right\}

4:

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\varnothing

5:

for all

(u,v)\in D_{1}

do

6:

w\leftarrow(\textsc{top}(v),\textsc{top}(v),\textsc{top}(u),\textsc{top}(u))

7:

\mathcal{S}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}\cup\{u\boxbar w\}

8:

return

\mathcal{S}
,
\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}:u\boxbar w\mapsto u\text{ for each }u\boxbar w\in\mathcal{S}

9:

\mathcal{S}
is a set of Wang tiles;

\eta:\Omega_{\mathcal{S}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is a shear conjugacy.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{6}=\theta(\textsc{Dominoes}^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{5}))
and

\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}
be defined as in
Equation (
7
)
where
\Omega_{6}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{6}}

and
\Omega_{5}=\Omega_{\mathcal{T}_{5}}
.
In SageMath, we compute

sage: # the following takes 6s with dancing_links, 3min 12s with Glucose, 22s with Gurobi
sage: T6,eta = T5.shear(radius=2, solver="dancing_links")

The map
\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}

is a bijection preserving the indices of tiles:

\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{llllll}0\mapsto\left(0\right),&1\mapsto\left(1\right),&2\mapsto\left(2\right),&3\mapsto\left(3\right),&4\mapsto\left(4\right),&5\mapsto\left(5\right),\\
6\mapsto\left(6\right),&7\mapsto\left(7\right),&8\mapsto\left(8\right),&9\mapsto\left(9\right),&10\mapsto\left(10\right),&11\mapsto\left(11\right),\\
12\mapsto\left(12\right),&13\mapsto\left(13\right),&14\mapsto\left(14\right),&15\mapsto\left(15\right),&16\mapsto\left(16\right),&17\mapsto\left(17\right),\\
18\mapsto\left(18\right),&19\mapsto\left(19\right),&20\mapsto\left(20\right),&21\mapsto\left(21\right),&22\mapsto\left(22\right),&23\mapsto\left(23\right),\\
24\mapsto\left(24\right),&25\mapsto\left(25\right),&26\mapsto\left(26\right),&27\mapsto\left(27\right),&28\mapsto\left(28\right).\end{array}\right.

and the resulting set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{6}
is:

\mathcal{T}_{6}=\left\{\raisebox{-48.36967pt}{\includegraphics[]{article2_T6_tiles.pdf}}\right\}

which we can work with because it has markers.

Part III
Desubstituting Wang tilings with markers again

8.
Desubstitution from
\Omega_{6}
to
\Omega_{12}
:

\Omega_{6}\xleftarrow{\omega_{6}}\Omega_{7}\xleftarrow{\omega_{7}}\Omega_{8}\xleftarrow{\omega_{8}}\Omega_{9}\xleftarrow{\omega_{9}}\Omega_{10}\xleftarrow{\omega_{10}}\Omega_{11}\xleftarrow{\omega_{11}}\Omega_{12}

Proposition 26
.

Let
\Omega_{6}
be the Wang shift defined above from
\mathcal{T}_{6}
.
There exist sets of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}_{7}
,
\mathcal{T}_{8}
,
\mathcal{T}_{9}
,
\mathcal{T}_{10}
,
\mathcal{T}_{11}
and
\mathcal{T}_{12}

together with their associated Wang shifts

\Omega_{7}
,
\Omega_{8}
,
\Omega_{9}
,
\Omega_{10}
,
\Omega_{11}
and
\Omega_{12}

and there exists a sequence of recognizable
2
-dimensional morphisms:

\Omega_{6}\xleftarrow{\omega_{6}}\Omega_{7}\xleftarrow{\omega_{7}}\Omega_{8}\xleftarrow{\omega_{8}}\Omega_{9}\xleftarrow{\omega_{9}}\Omega_{10}\xleftarrow{\omega_{10}}\Omega_{11}\xleftarrow{\omega_{11}}\Omega_{12}

that are onto up to a shift, i.e.,

\overline{\omega_{i}(\Omega_{i+1})}^{\sigma}=\Omega_{i}

for each
i\in\{6,7,8,9,10,11\}
.

Proof.

The proof is done by executing the function

\textsc{FindMarkers}(
)
followed by

\textsc{FindSubstitution}(
)
and repeating this process.
Each time
\textsc{FindMarkers}(
)
finds at least one subset of markers using a surrounding radius
of size at most 2.
Thus using Theorem
16
,
we may find a desubstitution of tilings.
The proof is done in SageMath
[
Sag19
]

using
slabbe
optional package

[
Lab19b
]
.

First, we desubstitute
\mathcal{T}_{6}
:

[[1, 6, 7, 8, 11, 12, 16, 17, 18, 19, 23, 26, 28],

[2, 9, 10, 20, 21, 22, 27]]
sage: M6 = [1, 6, 7, 8, 11, 12, 16, 17, 18, 19, 23, 26, 28]
sage: T7,omega6 = T6.find_substitution(M=M6, i=1, radius=1,
....: side="left", solver="dancing_links")

9.
Proofs of main result

In
[
Lab19a
]
the set of Wang tiles

\mathcal{U}=\left\{\raisebox{-28.45274pt}{\includegraphics[]{article2_U_tiles.pdf}}\right\}

was introduced. It was proved to be self-similar, aperiodic and minimal. In
this section, we prove that the desubstitution process we have done so far
starting from the Jeandel-Rao tiles
\mathcal{T}_{0}
leads to the self-similar
set of tiles
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 27
.

The set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and
\mathcal{U}
are equivalent.

Proof.

The correspondence between colors of
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and colors of
\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle A\mapsto 21030021300211300

\displaystyle K\mapsto 51160511

\displaystyle B\mapsto 21030021031211300

\displaystyle L\mapsto 60060

\displaystyle C\mapsto 21330020331210331

\displaystyle M\mapsto 51151511

\displaystyle D\mapsto 21033121331

\displaystyle N\mapsto 60060511

\displaystyle E\mapsto 21030020331210331

\displaystyle O\mapsto 51060

\displaystyle F\mapsto 21030021300

\displaystyle P\mapsto 51060511

\displaystyle G\mapsto 21030021300210300

\displaystyle H\mapsto 21330020331

\displaystyle I\mapsto 21033121331210331

\displaystyle J\mapsto 21030020331

provides the bijection between
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and
\mathcal{U}

which defines a bijection between
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}

and
\Omega_{12}
:

\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{12}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllll}0\mapsto\left(0\right),&1\mapsto\left(1\right),&2\mapsto\left(9\right),&3\mapsto\left(7\right),&4\mapsto\left(8\right),\\
5\mapsto\left(11\right),&6\mapsto\left(10\right),&7\mapsto\left(6\right),&8\mapsto\left(2\right),&9\mapsto\left(4\right),\\
10\mapsto\left(5\right),&11\mapsto\left(3\right),&12\mapsto\left(18\right),&13\mapsto\left(14\right),&14\mapsto\left(16\right),\\
15\mapsto\left(13\right),&16\mapsto\left(12\right),&17\mapsto\left(17\right),&18\mapsto\left(15\right).\end{array}\right.

∎

The self-similarity of
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is given by the following 2-dimensional
morphism:

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllll}0\mapsto\left(17\right),&1\mapsto\left(16\right),&2\mapsto\left(15,\,11\right),&3\mapsto\left(13,\,9\right),&4\mapsto\left(17,\,8\right),\\
5\mapsto\left(16,\,8\right),&6\mapsto\left(15,\,8\right),&7\mapsto\left(14,\,8\right),&8\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}6\\
14\end{array}\right),&9\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}3\\
17\end{array}\right),\\
10\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}3\\
16\end{array}\right),&11\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{r}2\\
14\end{array}\right),&12\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}7&1\\
15&11\end{array}\right),&13\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}6&1\\
14&11\end{array}\right),&14\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}7&1\\
13&9\end{array}\right),\\
15\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}6&1\\
12&9\end{array}\right),&16\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}5&1\\
18&10\end{array}\right),&17\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}4&1\\
13&9\end{array}\right),&18\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}2&0\\
14&8\end{array}\right).\end{array}\right.

We may gather the proof of the main result and its corollaries.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

(i) Proved in Proposition
18
.
(ii) Proved in
Proposition
22
.
(iii) Proved in
Corollary
25
.
(iv) Proved in Proposition
26
.
(v)
From Proposition
27
,
the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{12}
and
\mathcal{U}
are equivalent.
It was proved in
[
Lab19a
]

that
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is self-similar, aperiodic and minimal.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4
.

From Theorem
3
,

\Omega_{12}
is aperiodic and minimal.
From Lemma
7

and Lemma
9
,
we conclude that

\Omega_{11}
,

\Omega_{10}
,

\Omega_{9}
,

\Omega_{8}
,

\Omega_{7}
and

\Omega_{6}
are aperiodic and minimal.

From Corollary
25
,
the map
\eta:\Omega_{6}\to\Omega_{5}
is a
shear conjugacy. In particular, it is one-to-one, onto and
recognizable.
From Lemma
7

and Lemma
9
,
we conclude that
\Omega_{5}
is aperiodic and minimal.
∎

Proof of Corollary
5
.

From Proposition
22
,
the map
\jmath:\mathcal{T}_{5}\to\mathcal{T}_{4}
defines an embedding

\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
.
In particular, the map
\jmath
is recognizable.
From Lemma
7

and Lemma
9
,
we conclude that

X_{4}=\overline{\jmath(\Omega_{5})}^{\sigma}
is an aperiodic and minimal
subshift of
\Omega_{4}
.

From Proposition
18
and
Corollary
21
, we have
that
\omega_{i}:\Omega_{i+1}\to\Omega_{i}
is recognizable
for every
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.
From Lemma
7
and
Lemma
9
, we conclude that

X_{i}=\overline{\omega_{i}(X_{i+1})}^{\sigma}
is an aperiodic and minimal
subshift of
\Omega_{i}

for every
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

The proof of nonemptyness of
\Omega_{i}\setminus X_{i}
for every

i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
is postponed to Proposition
35
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

It follows from Theorem
3
.
∎

Proposition 28
.

Each tile of the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{i}
appears with positive
frequency in tilings in
X_{i}

for every
i\in\{0,1,\dots,12\}
.

Proof.

Let
v
be the positive right eigenvector of the incidence matrix of the
primitive substitution
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
. It can be checked that the incidence
matrix of
\left(\prod_{i=k}^{11}\omega_{i}\right)\rho
maps the vector
v

to a vector with positive entries for each
k\in\{0,1,\dots,11\}
where we
suppose
\omega_{4}=\jmath
and
\omega_{5}=\eta
to simplify the notation of
the product. This vector gives the relative frequency of each tile from

\mathcal{T}_{i}
in tilings in
X_{i}
.
∎

The frequencies of tiles in tilings of
X_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
is in
Table
1
.

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\text{Tiles}&\text{Frequency}\\
\hline\cr\raisebox{-8.53581pt}{

{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,1,0}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 0 L 17.72 17.72 L 35.43 35.43}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Table 1.
Frequency of Jeandel-Rao tiles in tilings in the proper subshift

X_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
in terms of the golden mean

\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
.

We now obtain independently the link between Jeandel-Rao aperiodic tilings and
the Fibonacci word. This link was used by Jeandel and Rao to prove aperiodicity
of their set of Wang tiles
[
JR15
]
.

Proposition 29
.

The language
\mathcal{L}\subset\{4,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of the sequences of horizontal strips of
height 4 or 5 in Jeandel-Rao tilings
X_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
is the language
of the Fibonacci word, the fixed point of
4\mapsto 5,5\mapsto 54
.

Proof.

Let
\psi:\Omega_{12}\to\{a,b,c,d\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
be the
2
-dimensional morphism
defined as

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\psi:\mathcal{T}_{12}&\to&\{a,b,c,d\}\\
t&\mapsto&\begin{cases}a&\text{ if }t\in\{12,13,14,15,16,17,18\},\\
b&\text{ if }t\in\{2,3,4,5,6,7\},\\
c&\text{ if }t\in\{8,9,10,11\},\\
d&\text{ if }t\in\{0,1\}.\end{cases}\end{array}

There exists a unique
2
-dimensional morphism

\tau:\psi(\Omega_{12})\to\psi(\Omega_{12})
which commute with the projection

\psi
that is
\psi\circ\tau=\omega_{12}\circ\psi
.
The morphism
\tau
is given by the rule

a\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&d\\
a&c\end{array}\right),\quad b\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&c\end{array}\right),\quad c\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
a\end{array}\right),\quad d\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a\end{array}\right).

Vertically, the Fibonacci morphism
a\mapsto ab,b\mapsto a
gives the structure of the
language of horizontal strips.
Then from Figure
18
,
we see that tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{12}
with index from 0 to 7 map to horizontal
strips of height
5+4
and that those with index from 8 to 18 map to
horizontal strips of height
5+4+5
.
The image of the language of the Fibonacci morphism
a\mapsto ab,b\mapsto a

by the map
a\mapsto 545,b\mapsto 54
is exactly the language of
the Fibonacci morphism
5\mapsto 54,4\mapsto 5
.
∎

The minimal subshift
X_{0}
is not equal to
\Omega_{0}
. Still we
believe that
X_{0}
is the only minimal subshift of
\Omega_{0}
and that

\Omega_{0}\setminus X_{0}
is a null set for any probability shift-invariant
measure on
\Omega_{0}
. The goal of the next two sections is to cover these
questions.

Part IV
Non-minimality of Jeandel-Rao tilings

10.
Eight tilings in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
fixed by the square of
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}

To understand Jeandel-Rao tilings
\Omega_{0}
we need to understand
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.
It was proved in
[
Lab19a
]

that the Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is minimal, aperiodic and self-similar
and that
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}

is an expansive recognizable morphism that is onto up
to a shift. The subshift
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is the hidden internal self-similar
structure of
\Omega_{0}
. In this section, we describe the fixed points of

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
which are helpful to define the tilings of
\Omega_{0}
that we
cannot describe by the image of morphisms.

The first two images of

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)

under the
2
-dimensional morphism
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
are:

\left(\begin{array}[]{r|r}17&13\\
\hline\cr 6&5\end{array}\right)\xrightarrow{\omega_{\mathcal{U}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{rr|rr}4&1&6&1\\
13&9&14&11\\
\hline\cr 15&8&16&8\end{array}\right)\xrightarrow{\omega_{\mathcal{U}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{rrr|rrr}17&8&16&15&8&16\\
6&1&3&7&1&2\\
14&11&17&13&9&14\\
\hline\cr 6&1&6&5&1&6\\
12&9&14&18&10&14\end{array}\right)

Therefore, the square of the morphism
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is prolongable on

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)

in all four quadrants
(since the original patch occurs in the center of the last one)
and there exist
a unique tiling

z:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{U}}

with
z=\omega_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}(z)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}

such that at the origin we have:

\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}z_{-\mathbf{e}_{1}}&z_{\mathbf{0}}\\
z_{-\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2}}&z_{-\mathbf{e}_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}17&13\\
6&5\end{array}\right).

Lemma 30
.

The square of the morphism
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
has eight distinct fixed points

z=\omega_{\mathcal{U}}(z)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
that can be generated from the following
values of
z
at the origin:

\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}z_{(-1,0)}&z_{\mathbf{0}}\\
z_{-(1,1)}&z_{(0,-1)}\end{array}\right)\in\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}9&14\\
8&16\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}9&14\\
1&6\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}17&13\\
16&15\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}17&13\\
6&5\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}16&15\\
3&7\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}10&12\\
9&14\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}16&13\\
2&4\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}10&14\\
11&17\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Figure 14.
The eight fixed point of
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
are generated by the
2\times 2
blocks
that belong to a recurrent strongly connected component of this graph.

Proof.

The proof is done by applying the morphism
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
on each of the
fifty
2\times 2
factors of the language of
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
already computed
in
[
Lab19a
]
. We get a graph shown
in Figure
14
. In the graph, there are 4 cycles of size
2 and no other strongly connected components.
Each of these 8 vertices correspond to a fixed point
z\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
of
the square of
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.
∎

Lemma 31
.

Among the eight fixed points of
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
,
only four contain a row made of tiles in
\{1,5,6\}

or a row made of tiles in
\{8,15,16\}
:
the fixed points
z=(\omega_{\mathcal{U}}^{2})^{\infty}(a)

generated by some

a\in\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}9&14\\
8&16\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}9&14\\
1&6\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}17&13\\
16&15\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}17&13\\
6&5\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Moreover, they contain exactly one of these row.

Proof.

The 8 fixed points are given in Lemma
30
.
Only 1, 5, 6 and 10 contain a row over
\{1,5,6\}^{*}
in their image under

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Only
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
1&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)
contain a row over
\{1,5,6,10\}^{*}
.
Since the exactly one row (the top one) of the images of
1,5,6
under

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is over
\{1,5,6\}^{*}
, this property is preserved under the
application of
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
. Similarly, we show that only

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
8&16\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
16&15\end{smallmatrix}\right)
generate fixed point with a row over
\{8,15,16\}^{*}
.
∎

Finally we illustrate in Figure
15
the language of
horizontal dominoes in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
as a Rauzy graph.

Figure 15.
The horizontal Rauzy graph for the tiles in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.
There is an arrow
a\to b
in the graph with
a,b\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{U}}
if and only if

a\odot^{1}b
is in the language of
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.

11.
Understanding
\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}

Recall that
X_{4}=\overline{\jmath(\Omega_{5})}^{\sigma}=\jmath(\Omega_{5})

as
\jmath(\Omega_{5})
is shift-invariant since
\jmath
is a uniform morphism
mapping each letter on a letter.
The goal of this section is to show that

\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}

is nonempty and give a conjecture on its structure.
Informally, we believe that all tilings in
\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}
are obtained
by sliding particular tilings in
X_{4}
along a horizontal fault line of 0’s or
1’s.

The morphism
\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{5}

is illustrated in
Figure
18
.
Recall that the application of the map
\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
replaces
horizontal colors 5 and 6 by 1 and 0 respectively.
Thus it can be seen on the figure that
horizontal fault lines of 0’s are present in the image of
tiles numbered 1, 5 and 6 and horizontal fault lines of 1’s are present in the images of
tiles numbered 8, 15 and 16. In the next lemma, we formalize this observation.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be an interval of integers. A Wang tiling
t:\mathbb{Z}\times K\to\mathcal{T}
has a
horizontal fault line
of color
a
if there exists
k\in K

such that
\textsc{bottom}(t(m,k))=a
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 32
.

Let
u:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{U}}

be a infinite horizontal row of some tiling in
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
.

(1)

\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
0
’s
if and only if
u\in\{1,5,6\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(2)

\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
1
’s
if and only if
u\in\{8,15,16\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

(i) If
u\in\{1,5,6\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
0
’s.
If
\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
0
’s, then
we see from Figure
18
that

u\in\{0,1,4,5,6,9,10\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In the horizontal language of
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
(see Figure
15
),
the tile 10 is preceded by 18 which has no fault line of 0’s and
the tile 9 is followed by 14 which has no fault line of 0’s.
Moreover, the tiles 0 and 4 are preceded by tile 2 which has no fault line of
0’s. We conclude that
u\in\{1,5,6\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(ii) If
u\in\{8,15,16\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
1
’s.
If
\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(u)

contains a horizontal fault line of
1
’s, then
we see from Figure
18
that

u\in\{8,13,15,16\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In the language of
\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
(see Figure
15
),
the tile 13 is always followed by 9 which has no fault line of 1’s.
We conclude that
u\in\{8,15,16\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

We can now use the result of the previous section on the fixed points of

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
to construct tilings in
\Omega_{4}
with horizontal fault lines.

Lemma 33
.

Let
a\in\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
8&16\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
1&6\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
16&15\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
and

z=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})^{2n}(a)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}
be the fixed point of

\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
with
a
at origin.
Let

y=\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(z)\in\Omega_{4}.

If
a\in\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
1&6\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
then
y
has a horizontal fault line of 0’s below the row
k=-1
.
If
a\in\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
8&16\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
16&15\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
then
y
has a horizontal fault line of 1’s below the row
k=-1
.

Proof.

The proof follows from
Lemma
31
and
Lemma
32
.
∎

Consider now two well-chosen tilings in
\Omega_{4}
:

\displaystyle y_{0}

\displaystyle=\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(z_{0})\in\Omega_{4}\quad\text{ with }\quad z_{0}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})^{2n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
1&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(z_{1})\in\Omega_{4}\quad\text{ with }\quad z_{1}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})^{2n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}9&14\\
8&16\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}.

For any Wang tiling
y:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{T}
,
we define
y^{+}
to be the tiling
y
restricted to the upper half-plane

\mathbb{Z}\times(\mathbb{N}-1)
and
y^{-}
to be the tiling
y
restricted to
the lower half-plane
\mathbb{Z}\times(-2-\mathbb{N})
:

y^{+}=y|_{\mathbb{Z}\times(\mathbb{N}-1)}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad y^{-}=y|_{\mathbb{Z}\times(-2-\mathbb{N})}.

It follows from Lemma
33
that
the tiling
y_{0}^{+}
(resp.
y_{1}^{+}
) is a tiling of the upper half-plane with
only color 0 (resp. 1) on the bottom. The tiling
y_{0}^{-}
(resp.
y_{1}^{-}
) is a
tiling of the lower half-plane with only color 0 (resp. 1) on the top.

Remark 34
.

Obviously, the tilings
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
agree on the upper half-plane

\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid n\geq 0\}
but surprisingly,
they also agree on the lower half-plane

\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid n\leq-3\}
.
We do not provide a proof of this here as it is not needed for the
next results.

Figure 16.
The tiling
x=y_{0}^{-}\odot^{2}\left(\sigma^{-\mathbf{e}_{1}}y_{0}^{+}\right)\in\Omega_{4}

illustrated in the window
[-8,7]\times[-5,4]
.
The image under the map
\jmath
of the marker tiles
shown in green in Figure
12
,
i.e., the tiles of
\mathcal{T}_{4}
with indices

\{0,3,4,5,13,14,15,23,24\}
,
are shown with green background here.
Those
kind of diagonal markers
appear on lines of slope
+1

but those diagonal lines are broken by the shift of the upper half by one unit
to the right. Hence
x\notin X_{4}
.

Proposition 35
.

The set
\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}
is nonempty.

Proof.

Let

x=y_{0}^{-}\odot^{2}\left(\sigma^{-\mathbf{e}_{1}}y_{0}^{+}\right)
.
Because of the horizontal fault line of 0’s, adjacent edges in the tiling
x

are still valid. Thus we have
x\in\Omega_{4}
is a valid Wang tiling.
But
x\notin X_{4}
.
Indeed,
any tiling in
X_{4}

is the image under
\jmath\circ\eta
of some tiling in
\Omega_{6}
.
The image of the marker tiles in
M_{6}

must appear on diagonal lines of slope
1
in the tiling
x

(recall Figure
12
).
The fact that the upper half-tiling
y^{+}
is shifted by one horizontal
unit breaks the property of diagonal line of markers in
x

(see Figure
16
).
Therefore
x\notin X_{4}
.
∎

We can construct an uncountable number of tilings in

\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}
.
For
i\in\{0,1\}
, let
Y_{i}^{+}
(resp.
Y_{i}^{-}
) be the subshift generated by the
orbit of
y_{i}^{+}
(resp.
y_{i}^{-}
) under the action of the horizontal shift

\sigma^{\mathbf{e}_{1}}
:

\displaystyle Y_{i}^{+}

\displaystyle=\textsc{Closure}\left(\left\{\sigma^{k\mathbf{e}_{1}}y_{i}^{+}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}\right),

\displaystyle Y_{i}^{-}

\displaystyle=\textsc{Closure}\left(\left\{\sigma^{k\mathbf{e}_{1}}y_{i}^{-}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}\right).

Any product
u^{-}\odot^{2}u^{+}
with
u^{-}\in Y_{i}^{-}
and
u^{+}\in Y_{i}^{+}
belongs to

\Omega_{4}
since they match along a horizontal fault line of
i
’s with

i\in\{0,1\}
.

Proposition 36
.

For
i\in\{0,1\}
,
the subset
of tilings in
\Omega_{4}
obtained by sliding half-plane along a horizontal fault line of
i
’s

\overline{Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}}^{\sigma}\subset\Omega_{4}

has measure 0.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be a shift invariant probability measure on
\Omega_{4}
, i.e.,

\mu(\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}X)=\mu(X)
for every measurable subset
X\subset\Omega_{4}

and every
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
i\in\{0,1\}
.
We have the following disjoint union

\overline{Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}}^{\sigma}=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma^{k\mathbf{e}_{2}}\left(Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}\right).

The union is disjoint since
it follows from Lemma
31
that
each of these tilings has at most one horizontal fault line
of
i
’s.
The set
Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}
is closed and thus measurable
with
\mu
-measure
\kappa
. Thus

\mu\left(\overline{Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}}^{\sigma}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\kappa

which is bounded only if
\kappa=0
.
We conclude that

\mu\left(\overline{Y_{i}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{i}^{+}}^{\sigma}\right)=0
.
∎

Conjecture 37
.

A tiling in
\Omega_{4}
has at most one horizontal fault line
and if it has one then it is a fault line of
0’s or 1’s.
In other words,

\Omega_{4}\setminus X_{4}=\left(\overline{Y_{0}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{0}^{+}}^{\sigma}\cup\overline{Y_{1}^{-}\odot^{2}Y_{1}^{+}}^{\sigma}\right)\setminus\overline{\{y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}}^{\sigma}.

where
y_{0}
and
y_{1}
are defined above and

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle=\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(z_{2})\in\Omega_{4}\quad\text{ with }\quad z_{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})^{2n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
16&15\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle y_{3}

\displaystyle=\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho(z_{3})\in\Omega_{4}\quad\text{ with }\quad z_{3}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})^{2n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}17&13\\
6&5\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}.

To prove that conjecture, which implies
Conjecture
2
,
we need to prove that the transducer associated to
the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}_{8}
(with each occurrence of 5 and 6 in horizontal colors
replaced by 1 and 0 respectively) is
synchronized
for some
definition of synchronization. Informally, if a word
v=v_{0}v_{1}\dots v_{k}=v^{\prime}_{0}v^{\prime}_{1}\dots v^{\prime}_{k}
is simultaneously the output of some computation

a_{0}\xrightarrow{u_{0}|v_{0}}a_{1}\xrightarrow{u_{1}|v_{1}}a_{2}\dots\xrightarrow{u_{k-1}|v_{k-1}}a_{k}

and the input of some computation

b_{0}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}_{0}|w_{0}}b_{1}\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}_{1}|w_{1}}b_{2}\dots\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1}|w_{k^{\prime}-1}}b_{k^{\prime}}

then
k=k^{\prime}
and
v_{i}=v^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i
with
1\leq i\leq k
,
then we say that the transducer is
synchronized
on the word
v
.
We need to show that the only arbitrarily large words for which the transducer

\mathcal{T}_{8}
is not synchronized are those of the form
00000\dots
or
111111\dots
.
Some partial results were found in that direction but they were insufficient to
prove the conjecture.

The morphism

\omega_{0}\,\omega_{1}\,\omega_{2}\,\omega_{3}:\Omega_{4}\to\Omega_{0}

is recognizable and onto up to a shift.

Figure 18.
The map

\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{5}

is recognizable and onto up to a shift. The map

\jmath:\Omega_{5}\to\Omega_{4}
then replaces horizontal colors 5 and 6 by 1 and
0, respectively, which creates horizontal fault lines of 0’s in the image of
tiles numbered 1, 5 and 6 and horizontal fault lines of 1’s in the images of
tiles numbered 8, 15 and 16.

Figure 19.
The map

\omega_{0}\,\omega_{1}\,\omega_{2}\,\omega_{3}\,\jmath\,\eta\,\omega_{6}\,\omega_{7}\,\omega_{8}\,\omega_{9}\,\omega_{10}\,\omega_{11}\,\rho:\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}\to\Omega_{0}
.
